18 


IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF COLORADO 


Civil Action No. 89-N-1159 
FEDERAL ELECTION COMMISSION, PLAINTIFF 


, 
v. 


COLORADO REPUBLICAN FEDERAL CAMPAIGN 
COMMITTEE, ET AL., DEFENDANTS. 


DEFENDANTS’ FIRST AMENDED COMPULSORY 
COUNTERCLAIM 


For its First Amended Counterclaim the Colorado 
Republican Federal Campaign Committe (“State 
Party”) and its Trasurer, Mr. Douglas L. Jones, incor- 
porate herein the averments and allegations of their 
Answer in this action and further aver as follows: 


Jurisdiction 


47. This counterclaim seeks to protect the right of 
the State Party and its adherents to speak and associ- 
ate freely with and for Party candidates for federal 
office. The rights asserted arise under the First 
Amendment to the United States Constitution and the 
Federal Election Campaign Act (“FECA”). Thus, this 
Court has subject matter jurisdiction pursuant to 28 
U.S.C. § 1331 (federal question) and § 1367 (supple- 
mental). The Supreme Court's earlier ruling in this case 


19 


recognized that jurisdiction exists over the actual 
controversy alleged in this counterclaim. 


Parties 


48. Counterclaim plaintiff, the State Party, is a 
political party. Its purposes are to advance Republican 
goals, vlaues, and principles in Colorado and to support 
Republican candidates for federal office from Colorado. 
It is funded by voluntary contributions from individuals 
who support its purposes and who wish to associate 
with other like minded individuals to speak out in 
support of Party candidates or in opposition to other 
candidates. 


49. Counterclaim plaintiff, Mr. Jones, is Treasurer of 
the State Party, a resident and citizen of Colorado, a 
registered Republican voter, and an adherent of the 
State Party. 


50. Counterclaim defendant, the Federal Election 
Commission (“FEC”), is an agency of the United States 
charged with enforcing the Federal Election Campaign 
Act (“FECA”). 


FECA Limits 


51. Section 441a(d) of FECA imposes a special limit 
on expenditures by parties in connection with federal 
elections. The Supreme Court in this action has held 
the § 441a(d) limit unconstitutional as applied to expen- 
ditures by the State Party that are independent of its 
candidates. The Supreme Court remanded the issue of 
whether, in light of that ruling, § 441a(d) limits coor- 
dinated expenditures by a party and, if so, whether 
those limits are constitutional. 


20) 


52. Section 44la(a)(2) of FECA limits a “multi- 
candidate political committee” to a maximum contribu- 
tion of $5,000 to any federal candidate or campaign in 
any year. Section 44la(a)(7)(B)(i) provides that an ex- 
penditure made “in cooperation, consultation, or con- 
cert, with, or at the request or suggestion of, a 
candidate, his authorized political committees, or their 
agents” is deemed to be a contribution. The FEC 
contends that, to the extent that a party’s expenditure 
in connection with a federal election is neither 
independent nor authorized by § 441a(d) or § 441a(a)(2) 
and § 44la(a)(7)(B)(i), or otherwise, those limits are 
referred to herein as the “FECA Limits.” This 
counterclaim seeks to invalidate all such FECA Limits 
to the extent they apply to expenditures by political 
parties. 


Injury 


54. By history and tradition the State Party, like 
other political parties, engages in joint political activity 
with its candidates for federal office. This joint activity 
includes “coordinated expenditures” which, as used 
herein, refers to expenditures that (i) are made in coop- 
eration, consultation, or concert with, or at the request 
or suggestion of, the Party’s federal candidates, their 
political committees, or their agents, (ii) are made using 
funds lawfully received from sources and in amounts 
allowed by FECA, and (iii) are made in connection with 
a general election for federal office. 


55. The FEC historically has enforced the FECA 
Limits against parties that make coordinated expendi- 
tures. Indeed, this proceeding commenced as such an 
action by the FEC. The FEC complaint herein asserts 
that the FECA Limits restrict the State Party’s coor- 


21 


dinated expenditures. The FEC’s general counsel has 
stated that consultations between a party and its candi- 
dates will disable the party from making independent 
expenditures in support of those candidates. Draft 
Advisory opinion 1996-30, FEC Agenda Document #96- 
85. The State Party faces a credible threat that the 
FECA Limits will be enforced against it if it makes 
expenditures that the FEC deems coordinated anu that 
exceed the FECA Limits. Violation of the FECA 
Limits threatens substantial civil sanctions or, in some 
circumstances, criminal penalties. 


56. The State Party’s coordinated expenditures 
serve to communicate the Party’s political views and 
other information to the Party’s adherents and to the 
voters of Colorado, including Mr. Jones. The State 
Party can communicate its message much more effi- 
ciently and effectively if it is able to coordinate with its 
candidates. Similarly, voters receive a clearer and 
more coherent picture if the State Party and its candi- 
dates can coordinate their speech. The coordination of 
expenditures is an important means of political associa- 
tion as well as of political speech. 


57. Since at least the general election of 1986, the 
State Party has been ready, willing, and able to make 
coordinated expenditures in excess of the FECA 
Limits, and it is and will remain ready, willing, and able 
to do so in 1998, 2000, and beyond. However, the State 
Party has been and remains chilled and deterred from 
making coordinated expenditures in excess of the 
FECA Limits by the credible threat that the FECA 
Limits will be enforced by the FEC or by a private 
complainant. 


22 


58. The FEC’s general counsel has asserted a broad, 
though no clearly defined, understanding of a 
meaning of coordination. Even a successful defense . 
an expenditure as noncoorc nated is likely to be 
burdensome and intrusive. The State Party has been 
and is chilled and deterred from engaging In activity 
that is not coordinated but that may be so characterized 
by the FEC or private complainants. Voters have thus 
been denied the ability to receive such speech and the 
ability of State Party adherents to associate for such 
purposes has been impaired. 


59. The FECA Limits have the present and 
immediate effect of chilling and deterring the State 
Party's planning for coordinated speech. Also, the 
State Party’s present speech is chilled and deterred by 
the risk that expenditures later will be held to be 
coordinated and counted against the Party’s FECA 
Limits for the next elections. 


60. The injuries thus suffered by the State Party, its 
adherents, and the voters of Colorado have been and 
are serious and irreparable. The injury will increase in 
severity as the 1998 election cycle advances, and 
further important opportunities to speak on vital 
political matters will be irrevocably lost (as already has 
occurred) until and unless relief is granted by the 
courts. The State Party and Mr. Jones have no 
adequate remedy at law. 


Severability 


61. Contrary to the FEC’s position, when FECA is 
properly construed in light of the Supreme Court s 
earlier ruling in this case, it imposes no FECA Limits 
on coordinated expenditures of the State Party or of 


23 


other similar parties. The FEC’s attempts and intent 


to impose such limits are unconstitutional, unlawful, 
and void. 


62. Section 441a(d) imposes a unitary limit that does 
not distinguish between independent and coordinated 
expenditures. The Supreme Court’s ruling that the © 
limits imposed by § 441a(d) are unconstitutional as 
applied to independent expenditures invalidates the 
entire limit provision of § 441a(d) in all applications. 


63. The invalidity of the limits imposed by Section 
441a(d) does not invalidate the provision exempting 
parties such as the State Party from other FECA 
Limits. Alternatively, those other limits are not 
severable from the unconstitutional limit imposed by 
§ 441a(d) and likewise are invalid. 


First Amendment Violations 


64. Contrary to the FEC’s position, the First 
Amendment forbids the government to limit the State 
Party’s coordinated expenditures. The FEC’s attempts 
and intent to impose or enforce any limit on such 


coordinated expenditures are unconstitutional, unlawful 
and void. 


65. To the extent that FECA Limits established by 
§ 441la(d) or § 441a(a)(2) and § 441a(a)(7)(B)(i), or other- 
wise, survive the Supreme Court’s earlier ruling in this 
case, each such limit violates the First Amendment (i) 
facially, (ii) as applied to political parties such as the 
State Party, and (iii) as interpreted by the FEC. 


a. No compelling governmental interest justifies 
limiting the ability of the State Party to coordinate with 


24 


its candidates in the expenditure of lawfully received 
funds that the State Party could expend independently 
in support of those same candidates. In particular, such 
coordination does not give rise to compelling concerns 
about corruption, nor did Congress impose any FECA 
Limits on political parties because of corruption 
concerns. 


b. If any such compelling interest were to be 
established, it could be adequately served by other less 
intrusive and burdensome means than the FECA 
Limits. 


¢. The FECA Limits are impermissibly vague and 
overbroad and are not narrowly tailored to any com- 
pelling interest. 


d. The FECA Limits were imposed for the uncon- 
stitutional purposes of reducing the amount of political 
speech by political parties and equalizing the amount of 
speech by competing viewpoints. 


e. The unconstitutional effects and nature of the 
FECA Limits are compounded by the low dollar limits 
established. 


WHEREFORE the State Party and Mr. Jones re- 
quest the following relief: 


A. A declaratory judgment pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 
§$ 2201 that the State Party has the right to make 
unlimited expenditures from lawfully received contri- 
butions in support of its candidates for federal office, 
and that any limits that FECA purports to impose are 
invalid and void. 


25 


B. Appropriate further relief pursuant to 28 U.S 
§ 2202 and the Court’s equitable powers, ra 
necessary a preliminary injunction to protect the State 
Party and Mr. Jones from further irreparable injury 
pending the final outcome of this case. 


‘s = An award of costs and fees and such other relief is

12. “The chief function parties perform is to contest 
elections.” Corrado Rpt. at 2. Their distinctive objec- 
tive is to build legislative ma Jorities and help to orga- 
nize government. Id. at 4, 21. “Parties seek to commu. 
nicate their views and coalesce diverse interests behind 
policy positions predominantly through electoral 
activity. Party organizations concentrate a substantial 
share of their resources and activities on political 
campaigning. They play a central role in recruiting 
candidates, raising campaign funds, com municating 
information to voters, and encouraging citizens to vote. 
They develop messages and platforms designed to 
appeal to broad-based majorities in an effort to win 
elections and build legislative majorities. These 
messages provide voters with outlooks that help them 
comprehend the choices available and structure their 
decision making. In particular, the parties provide 

citizens, especially those who identify themselves as 
party supporters, with perceptual frameworks and 

voting cues that help them assess candidates and 

issues.” Id. at 1; accord Alexander Rpt. at 9; § 421(16) 
(defining “political party” as “an association, com- 

mittee, or organization which nominates a candidate 


for election to any Federal office whose name appears

on the election ballot as the candidate of such 
association, committee, or organization”).

14. “American political parties are among the oldest 
in the world, but their power has waned in the last half 
century. Historically, the parties suffered when civil 
service and professionalization replaced patronage; 
when primaries took candidate selection out of their 
hands; when government began to provide social ser- 
vices the urban party organizations used to attract the 
allegiance of voters; when television focused dramati- 
cally on the candidate independent of his or her party; 
when immigration declined, the movement to the 
suburbs accelerated, and urban party organizations 
lost some of their support and reasons for being; when 
education led to the ethic to vote for the ‘better person’, 
[sic] however perceived, leading to increased ticket- 
splitting.” Id. at 9. “The diminished power of the 
parties makes the game of politics more difficult for the 
[powerless electorates] to play and win. Finally, the 
continued decline of the parties would rob the political 
system of an important building of majorities. . . . 
With weakened parties how are we to find majorities in 
a fragmented politics?” Frank Sorauf, Party Politics 
in America at 430. 


The FEC ADMITS sentences one through eight are 
based on the opinion of expert witnesses in this case. 
As opinion, the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR 
DENY; the reports speak for themselves.

Party Finances - Hard Money, Soft Money, Disclosure 


17. American political parties “have no money of 
their own. ” Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. at 37; see Corrado Rpt. 
at 8. In contrast to economic entities, their ability to 
spend in support of their candidates depends entirely 
on their ability to attract voluntary contributions 

which, in turn, depends directly on their level of popu- 
lar support. See Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. at 37, 42. Party 
conduct disapproved of by the public translates into 
reduced contributions. Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. at 42. Pr- 
fessor Corrado notes, as an example of this, that ap- 

proximately 200,000 donors to the Republican National 

Committee (“RNC”) ceased giving during the Bush 

administration. Corrado Dep. at 71. The RNC realized 

that it was not appealing to its base. Id. By being 

responsive to this indicator, the RNC “not only brought 

back those 200,000 small donors, but added in 200 000 

new smaller donors to give themselves a very broad 

base of support, that was the base of the strength they 
had entering [the] ‘96 election.” Id. at 72. A cor- 
poration, in contrast, is lawfully enabled by its 


40) 


corporate form to amass “large aggregations of wealth” 
based on approval in the marketplace rather than the 
public’s support of its policy positions or its political 
contributions. Id. at 91. 


The FEC ADMITS the facts described in this paragraph 
but the characterizations of those facts are the opinions 
of expert witnesses in this case. As opinion, the FEC IS 
NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the reports 


speak for themselves. 


18. “Hard money” is the common term for the 
limited, disclosed funds parties raise from individuals 
and political committees in conformity with FECA 
limits. FECA currently permits parties to use hard 
money in restricted amounts for expenditures “in 
connection with” a federal election. § 441a(d). Only 
hard money may be used by parties to expressly 
advocate the election or defeat of federal candidates . 


Id. 

The FEC ADMITS that “hard money” is a common 
term. The remainder of this paragraph contains state- 
ments or conclusions of law which the FEC IS NOT 
REQUIRED TO EITHER ADMIT OR DENY. 


19. Parties may accept hard money contributions 
only from specified sources and in limited amounts. 
Individuals may contribute an aggregate amount of 
$25,000 per year to federal candidates and campaigns, 
and federal and state parties. § 441a(a)(3). Of this, no 
more than $20,000 may go to any national party, and 


41 


no more than $5,000 may go to any state party. § 
441a(a)(1)(B), (C). Corporations and unions may not 
contribute hard money. § 441b(a). However, their 
political action committees (“PACs”) may contribute 
up to nae per year to a national party and $5,000 
per year to a state party with no a imi 

$4410(a)(2XB),(C). ara 


This paragraph contains statements or conclusions of 


law which the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO EITHER 
ADMIT OR DENY. 


20. “Soft money” is the term commonly applied to 
contributions given to political parties from sources or 
m amounts that FECA does not regulate. These funds 
may be spent for state election activity and non-election 
activity such as issue advertising, “get out the vote” 
campaigns, and other party building activities. 
$ 431(8)(B). Soft money may not be used “in connec- 
tion with” federal elections and hence it may not be 
used for coordinated or independent expenditures. 
Thus, soft money expenditures are not at issue in this 
case. Corrado Dep. at 35-36. 


The FEC ADMITS that “soft money” is a common term. 
The remainder of this paragraph contains statements or 
conclusions of law which the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED 
TO EITHER ADMIT OR DENY. The last sentence is a 
statement of the Colorado Party’s position in this 
litigation, which the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO 
EITHER ADMIT OR DENY. 


42 


21. Political issue advocacy describes political speech 
that may mention specific political parties or even 
candidates but does not “expressly advocate” the elec- 
tion or defeat of a clearly identified federal candidate. 
Buckley, 424 U.S. at 42; Federal Election Commn v. 
Christia ay te Motes rk, Inc., 110 F.3d 1049, 1051 -53, 
62 (4th Cir. 1997). “Issue ads” are an increasingly 
controversial phenomena due to several factors. First, 
the large amounts of money spent by organizations and 
interest groups in the 1996 elections piqued the public 
interest. Second, despite the impact these ads can have 
on federal elections, because they are not deemed to be 
“in connection with a federal election,” no organization 
is exempted from making these ads, there are no limits 
on how much can be raised or spent, and there are no 
requirements that sponsoring organizations disclose 
the sources of their funding, or where or how it was 
spent. Nonetheless, issue ads are also not at issue in 
this case. 


The first sentence is a statement of law, which the FEC 
IS NOT REQUIRED TO EITHER ADMIT OR DENY. 
Sentences two through four are unsubstantiated by any 
evidence in the record; therefore, the FEC IS NOT 
REQUIRED TO EITHER ADMIT OR DENY. Further- 
more, they represent some "inknown person’s opinions, 
which the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO EITHER

the Colorado Party’s legal position in this case, which 
the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO EITHER ADMIT OR

at 45.

Parties And Their Candidates 


28. “It is a natural relationship for parties and 
candidates to work together toward the common goal of 
winning elections.” Alexander Rpt. at 26. FECA 
recognizes that candidates for federal office appear “on 
the election ballot as the candidate of such [political 
party].” § 431(16). The Supreme Court plurality in 
this case recognize the intimate relationship shared by 
political parties and the campaigns of party candi- 
dates. Colorado, 116 S. Ct. at 2317- 19; see also id. at 
2323 (Kennedy, J. concurring in the judgment and 
dissentir ) in part) (noting that the fates of political 
parties and their candidates are “inextricably inter- 
twined”). “This close relationship does not increase the 
risk of corruption, but rather encourages party com- 
mit.ees to support with funding candidates who are 
faced with millionaire opponents, negative independent 
expenditures or negative issue advertising by indepen- 
dent groups.” Alexander Rpt. at 25. 


The FEC ADMITS that the first and fourth sentences 
are based on the opinion of Prof. Alexander. As 
opinion, the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR 
DENY; his report speaks for itself. The second and 
third sentences are statements or conclusions of law 
which the FEC NEITHER ADMITS NOR DENIES; the 
statute speaks for itself. 


29. The FEC long took the position that an expendi- 
ture by a party in connection with a federal election 


49 


was unavoidably an expenditure coordinated with the 
partys candidate. FEC Advisory Opinion 1988-22 
Fed. Election Camp. Fin. Guide (CCH) 4 5932 p. 
11,471 n. 4 (1988); see FEC Advisory Opinion 1985-14, 
Fed. Election Camp. Fin. Guide (CCH) 4 5819, p. 
11,486 n. 4 (1985) (“Party political committees are 
incapable of making independent expenditures.”); FEC 
Advisory Opinion 1984-15, Fed. Election Camp. Fin. 
Guide (CCH) 4 5766 p. 11,070 n. 2 (1984) (same); see 
also 11 CFR § 110.7(b)(4) (forbidding national and 
state party committees from making “independent 
expenditures in connection with the general election 
campaign[s]” for federal office) (repealed in the 
aftermath of Colorado). In making its ruling in this 
case the Supreme Court held that it was possible for 
parties to make independent expenditures that advo- 
cate the election or defeat of a candidate, so long as 


me is no coordination in fact. Colorado, 116 S. Ct. at 
2315. nm 


This paragraph contains statements or conclusions of 


law which the FEC is not required to EITHER ADMIT 
OR DENY. ) 


Independent And Coordinated Spending 


30. Independent expenditures are unnatural for par- 
ties and create an artificial separation of the party and 
its candidate. Alexander Rpt. at 25. Accordingly, a 
party now faces the quandary of how it can communi- 
— with yeep “while at the same time not 

municate when providing independent e )- 
twres on behalf of its candidate or roar myer 
date’s opponent.” Id. at 26. The task of assuring that 


50 


such expenditures are not coordinated with a party's 
candidate has proven cumbersome, inefficient, and ex- 
pensive. Corrado Rpt. at 35-37; Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. at 
44: Bain Dep. at 46-47. “Independent expenditures ave 
not natural acts for parties. The normal workings of 
parties include: recruiting candidates and writing plat- 
forms, raising funds to support those [] candidates and 
orga nizing voters.” Alexander Rpt. at 25. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the 
opinion of expert witnesses in this case. As opinion, the 
FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the 


reports speak for themselves. 


31. Assuring that expenditures are “independent” 
requires a party, whose natural role is to work closely 
with its candidates, “to stay at a safe distance from the 
candidate and the candidate's campaign plans and 
strategies.” Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. at 40. This, of course 
i hibits “a unified message.” Id.; see Corrado Rpt. at 
32. The current system of unlimited independent 
spending and severely restricted coordi nated spending 
places “enormous regulatory burdens” on parties and 
creates “a sloppy area of the law.” Corrado Dep. at 54- 
56. Moreover, even where care is taken to assure that 
the expenditures are. not coordinated, the inevitable 
close ties between party and candidate mean there is a 
constant threat that the FEC, perhaps at the instigation 
of the opposing party or candidate, will challenge the 
independence of the expenditure. Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. 
at 46; Bain Dep. at 47. : 


This paragraph contains legal conclusions and the 


opinion of expert witnesses in this case. As legal con-

clusions and opinion, the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO 
ADMIT OR DENY; the reports speak for themselves. 


52. To avoid claims of coordination, each of the ma- 
jor party organizations in 1996 established a “separate 
entity that was solely responsible for making inde- 
pendent expenditures. Each party had to rent and fur- 
nish an office, hire staff, and pay other administrative 
costs. They had to engage additional consulting serv- 
ices and duplicate many of the functions already being 
undertaken by other party offices.” Corrado Rpt. at 36- 
37; see Barbour Dep. at 100. This process was “cumber- 
some, complicated, and time-consuming.” Corrado 
Rpt. at 37. These complications and arrangements 
“tend to isolate the party . . . from candidates [and] 
destroy[] the healthy relationship among candidates 
and parties whose labels they use.” Alexander Rpt. at 
25. Moreover, such independent expenditures “do not 
qualify for the lowest unit rates on the purchase of 
broadcasting time,” as coordinated expenditures would. 
Corrado Rpt. at 37; see 47 U.S.C. § 315(b) (“The charges 
made for the use of any broadcasting station by any 
person who is a legally qualified candidate for any 
public office in connection with his campaign for 
nomination for election, or election, to such office shall 
not exceed . . . the lowest unit charge of the station for 
the same class and amount of time for the same period. 

9; 47 CFR § 73.1942 (1997) (implementing and 
defining requirement that broadcasters sell advertising 
time to candidates at the lowest unit charge of the 
station); see also CBS, Inc. v. FCC, 453 U.S. 367, 379 
(1981) (discussing the broadcast provisions of FECA, 
the Court stated that “all three measures, while 
differing in approach, were ‘intended to increase a

Parties and PACs Comparison 


44. “Political parties have some surface similarities 
with PACs and other groups: they all act in the 
political arena, they all seek to elect candidates of their 
choice, they all spend money, they all want some policy 
outcome. But parties have one vital difference from 
interest groups: while the various interest groups (and 
their PACs) usually have one specific goal or concern, 


political parties represent an amalgam or coalition of 


interests and goals; moreover, the purpose of parties ts 
to gain control of government, rather than to pursue 


59 


single goals, as PACs do.” Alexander Rpt. at 17. Can- 
didates run as the nominees of their party. § 421(16). 


The FEC ADMITS that the first and second sentences 
are based on the opinion of Prof. Alexander. As 
opinion, the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR 
DENY; the report speaks for itself. The FEC ADMITS 
the third sentence. To the extent that this paragraph is 
a statement of law, no response is required. 


45. “Political parties seek to win elections by bring- 
ing together coalitions of [various individuals and 
interest] groups and by articulating issues that will 
resonate with voters. Political scientists generally 
value this activity as important consensus building in 
a diverse democracy.” Alexander Rpt. at 17. FEC 
expert witness Sorauf notes that while “/o]ther non- 
party organizations may raise funds, organize endorse- 
ments, and recruit workers for some candidate's cam- 
paign!,] parties are occupied with contesting elections 
ina way and to a degree that the other political orga- 
nizations are not. Indeed, their names and symbols ave 
the ones the states recognize for inclusion on most 


ballots.” Party Politics in America at 16. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the 
opinion of experts in this case. As opinion, the FEC IS 
NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY. 


46. Another difference between parties and “special 
interest groups or PACs [is that] parties must appeal 
to majorities in the electorate if they are to win: and 


60 


unlike single issue organizations, they must win races 
if they are to govern. Parties thus give coherence to 
American polities. Parties have been a unifying force 
in this system, building coalitions and making mass 
democracy possible.”  Alewander Rpt. at 18. As 
Professor Sorauf explains, parties have the “abil ity to 
make hard political choices, to pursue a collective 
electoral strategy that will maximize—or will work 
toward maximizing—the number of victories for a 
given expenditure of money.” Frank Sorauf, Money in 
American Elections 142-43 (1988); see Corrado Rpt. at 


PJ -2 2. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the 
opinion of experts in this case. As opinion, the FEC IS 
NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY.

speak out in support of their political tnterests. To do 
this parties must “communicate as widely as possible, 
and [they] do[] this in part on [their] own and in part 
through [their] candidates. In lieu of unlimited party 
support, PACs representing special interests compete 
for the candidates’ attention. It enhances democracy to 
encourage the broader interests parties represent, than 
the narrower interests of PACs.” Alexander Rpt. at 18. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the

NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the report and

that the AFL-CIO and the Christian Coalition are terms 
that do not appear on the ballot, but do not have 
sufficient evidence to admit or deny that voters do or do 
not make electoral decisions based on whether a 
candidate is the “AFL-CIO candidate” or the “Christian 


Coalition candidate.” 


Public Perception And Reality 


49. According to the FEC’s proffered expert witness 
on public opinion, Clyde Wilcox, no evidence exists 
indicating that the public is aware of, much less con- 
cerned about, coordinated expenditures. Wilcox Rpt. at 
1-2. He says that present public attitudes reflect pro- 


found ignorance of how federal election laws work. Id. 


62 


at 2. He notes that most Americans are not even aware 
that FECA limits the abilities of corporations or 
private citizens to donate their own funds directly to 
candidates. Id. FEC witness Wirth agrees: “I don't 
think anybody has any idea that [a coordinated limit] 
exists.” Wirth Dep. at 69. Maria Cino similarly testi- 
fies that “99 percent of the American public” lacked 
any idea about limits on party spending in support of 
their candidates. Cino Dep. at 123-24; accord Williams 
Decl. at 9 11. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the 
opinion of experts in this case. As opinion, the FEC IS 
NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the reports and 


testimony speak for themselves. 


50. Professor Sorauf concurs that public opinion 
concerning campaign finance issues lacks touch with 
“reality,” 94 Colum L. Rev. at 1359, and asserts that, in 
the public perception, “[m]oney has come to 
explain too much too easily in the new epistemology. . 
Inside Campaign Finance, at 24. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the 
opinion of Prof. Sorauf. As opinion, the FEC IS NOT 
REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the law review 
article speak for itself. 


51. Professor Sorauf attributes the public’s misun- 
derstandings in large part to the mass media, noting 


that “its mythmaking has been especially important.” 


94 Colum._L. Rev. at 1356. He points out that, in 
addition to the “worldviews of Populism and Pro- 


63 


gressivism” characteristic of reporters, the problem 
flows from “the very nature of the journalistic enter- 
prise: the need for simplicity, for personalization, for 
conflict, for change and novelty, and for bad or 
shocking news.” Id. at 1356 n. 23. He also votes that 
stories about campaign finance tend to serve to fill » p 
“a slow news day,” observing that “an inordinate pro- 
portion of stories on campaign finance appear ov 
Sunday television or in Monday morning newspapers.” 
Iuside Campaign Finance, at 162. | | 


The FEC ADMITS that this is this paragraph is based 
on the opinion of Prof. Sorauf. As opinion, the FEC Is 
NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the law review 
article and book speak for themselves.

Coordinated Expenditures Are Not Corrupting 


». The coordinated expenditure “limits were enacted 
with a view to reducing overal! spending, not because 
coordinated expenditures were in any way considered 
to be corrupting.” Alevander Rpt. at 14-15. “In the 
decades since 1974, when coordinated expenditures 
were allowed for both presidential and congressional 
campaigns, there has not been any dispute relating to 
them, no charges of corruption or the appearance 
thereof, so clearly coordinated expenditures could be 
increased substantially or unlimited without fear of 
corruption.” Alevander Rpt. at 14. 


The first sentence is the opinion of an expert on a 
conclusion of law for which no response is necessary. 
The FEC ADMITS that second sentence is based on the 
opinion of Prof. Alexander. As opinion, the FEC IS 


NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the report 
speaks for itself.

Ho. The writings of FEC expert witness Sorauf

behavior.” 94 Colum. L. Rev. at 1250.

66. The FEC's expert witnesses make no claim that 
quid pro quo corruption is a focus of concern for 
purposes of this case. They assert instead that the 
concept of “corruption” must be “expanded” to include 
the risk that contributors of “large sums of money” 
may obtain “access” to candidates and elected officials 
that gives them “extra opportunities to persuade.” 
Sorauf/Krasno Rpt. at 3-8. Thus, when the FEC’s 
witnesses speak of “corruption,” they are using that 
term in this much watered down sense, never adopted 
hy the Supreme Court, to refer to obtaining such 


opportunities for access and to persuade. Corrado Dep.

69. Access to political party candidates and office-

persons who are unwilling or unable to make large 
contributions cannot obtain access in myriad other 
ways. Rather, the reality is that “many [individuals] 
groups and organizations who don't give contributions” 
still have access to members of Congress and their 
staffs. Corrado Dep. at 238-39. Professor Corrado, for 
example, notes that because of his background and area 
of expertise he has frequent “access.” Id. 242-42.

77. The witnesses agree, without exception, that the 
most important factor in determining the extent of 
party funding of races is the marginality or com- 
petitiveness of the race. E.g., Bain Dep. at 31-22; 
Barbour Dep. at 100, 115; Cino Dep. at 101, 103; Wirth 
Dep. at 60. Thus, “[iJn [California's] ‘close’ [state 
legislative] elections, those where the challenger won at 
least 40% of the vote, the average contribution in 198s 
from party sources was $213,852 to challengers, but 
only $54,460 to incumbents. Challengers received 30.56 

of their funds from party sources, while incumbents 
received 11.6%.” Scully, at ¥ 276. 

The first sentence is a characterization by an unknown 

person of the evidence in this case and as such no 

response is necessary. The Commission objects to 

proffered facts from California Prolife Counc! 

Political Action Comm. v. Scully, No. Civ. 8-96-1965, 

1997 WL 7175, at *15 n. 1 (E.D. Cal. Jan. 6, 1998). The 

Commission was not a party to that suit, and thus has 

not been given an opportunity to review, challenge or 

respond to the evidentiary bases on which the findings 


were made. Furthermore, the Colorado Party has not 


78 


submitted the evidence from the Scully case to this 
Court. We also note that Scully involved a challenge to 
a California state law which imposed restrictions on the 
amount non- federal candidates may raise and spend in 
their non-federal campaigns. Accordingly, the evidence 
introduced in Scully, and the corresponding findings of 
fact. do not relate to federal campaigns, federal can- 


didates, federal political committees, or the Colorado 


Party. 


78. “Party coordinated expenditures are especially 
important because parties are the only source of 
funding that mostly benefits non-incumbents. As 
political scientists have noted for years, one of the 
major problems in the campaign finance system is the 
inability of challengers to raise the monies needed to 
wage viable campaigns.” Corrado Rpt. at 25. Cha- 
llengers “often find it difficult to raise the funds needed 
to make their views known to the electorate.” Id. at 29. 
“Challengers therefore look to party committees for 
assistance in overcoming the significant financial 
disadvantages they experience in most elections. Party 
money is the only major source of funds in the political 
system that challengers can rely on to help counter the 
overwhelming resource advantage incumbents en joy as 
a result of their greater success in fundraising [and] 
the privileges that accompany their position in gov- 
ernment, such as the franking privilege.” Id. at 26. 


The FEC ADMITS that this paragraph is based on the 
opinion of Prof. Corrado. As opinion, THE FEC IS NOT 


79 


REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the report speaks 
for itself. 


79. By contrast, “[p]arty resources are not a major 
source of funding for incumbents.” Id. at 22. Asa 
general rule, incumbents have an established campaign 
organization, campaign experience, name recognition 

and credibility in raising funds directly, advanta es 
that most challengers lack. Id. at 26. Access to PAC 
money alone strongly favors incumbents. “[I]n 1996 
PACs gave an average of over $950,000 to Senate 
incumbents, more than three times the average amount 
they gave Senate challengers.” Id. Similarly, in 1996 
"PACs gave an average of close to $300,000 to House 
incu mbents, which was more than five times the 
amount given to challengers and approximately five 
times more than the amount that national and state 
parties combined were legally allowed to spend 
($61,820) on behalf of such challengers.” Id. at 26-27 

In many instances, parties would be willing to spend 
more to assist their candidates, but are prevented from 
doing so because of [the] federal ceilings [at issue in 
this case]. : Id. at 27. Accordingly, “[i]n addition to 
spending disproportionately on marginal, competitive 
races, the parties time their spending to assure its 


strategic value.” Money in American Elections 141. 
The FEC ADMITS the first five sentences in this para- 
graph, but notes that these are explicitly generalized 
facts that may not be accurate for all candidates. The 
last two sentences are opinions, which the FEC IS NOT 
REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY. 


SO 


80. One of FEC expert Sorauf’s books documents the 
lack of funding for challengers: “What was a 1.5 tol 
~ advantage over their challengers in receipts in 1978 
became a 3.7 to 1 spread by 1990.” Frank Sorauf, 
Inside Campaign Finance 16 (1992). “In 1980 the 
average House major-party challenger running against 
an incumbent in the general election spent $100,408 in 
the entire campaign; by 1990 that figure was only 
$109,377, and only $54,503 in 1980 dollars. The average 
incumbent spent $165,599 in 1980 and $399,310 in 1990 
($199,197 in 1980 dollars).” Id. at 174. In addition to 
this funding advantage, incumbents’ “advantages of 
office,” including the “postal fra nk, their easy access to 
the media, their district offices, and their staffs for ‘ser- 
vicing’ constituents, all have grown in recent decades.” 
Id. at 176. Parties can potentially present a partial 
counterbalance for the challenger. See Billings Decl. at 
q 18. Professor Sorauf notes that, in contrast to other 
election funding sources, parties tend to direct their 
support to nonincumbents. Mo D rica) - 
tions 141. Accordingly, “to the extent [that] you limit 
the role of the party, you basically are protecting the 
good old boy network, as opposed to reaching out [to 
challengers].” Coelho Dep. at 117. 


The FEC ADMITS the amounts described, but the 
remainder of sentences one through four and sentence 
six are based on the opinion of Prof. Sorauf. As opinion, 
the FEC IS NOT REQUIRED TO ADMIT OR DENY; the 
report speaks for itself. [book] To the extent this 
paragraph restates facts supported by Prof. Sorauf’s 
book, the FEC ADMITS. The FEC DENIES that

THE CONGRESSIONAL FORUM 
AND 
HOUSE COUNCIL 


PROGRAMS OF THE NATIONAL REPUBLICAN 
CONGRESSIONAL COMMITTEE 


The Congressional Forum and House Council programs 
of the National Republican Congressional Committee 
have been developed to open the channels of com- 
munication between the business community, PACs, 
and Republican House Members. The programs in- 
volve its participants in the policy-making process 
through an unprecedented level of dialogue with House 
Republicans and party leaders. ~ 


These programs offer an unprecedented number of 
quality events which allow you to develop important 
relationships not only with Republican Members of 
Congress, but also with others in the business 
community. Regular meetings permit Congressional 
Forum and House Council members to voice their 
opinions and exchange information regarding the issues 
directly affecting their industry. 


THE CONGRESSIONAL FORUM 


$15,000 Annually for Individuals and PACs 
$20,000 Annually for Corporations 


The Congressional forum is the N RCC’s major do- 
nor program for the business community. This 
program has been designed to give its members an 
intimate setting to develop stronger working rela- 
tionships with the new Republican Congressional 
majority. 


BENEFITS OF MEMBERSHIP: 


¢ Monthly private dinners with the Chairmen and 
Republican Members of key Congressional 
Committees. 


e Private dinner with Speaker Newt Gingrich and 
the GOP House Leadership. 


e Private dinner with House Committee Chairmen. 


e An invitation to the Chairman’s Skeet and Trap 
Shoot. 


e VIP Preference at House Council and NRCC 
events. 


The most important thing you can do to support 
Republicans is to support the NRCC. Membership 
(es iv ( | »§ 


THE HOUSE COUNCIL 


$5,000 Annually for Individuals and PACs 
$10,000 Annually for Corporations 


The House Council is the NRCC’s lower-dollar 
donor program for the business community. The 
program strives to open the lines of communication 
between Republican Members of Congress, key- 
staff, and the business and PAC community. 
Through its briefings and events this program pro- 
vides an unprecedented level of dialogue and 
information for its members.

CHAIRMAN’S FOUNDATION NEWS

Senator Al D’Amato 
Chairman 


September 27, 1995 
FOUNDATION MEETING NOTES 


The Chairman’s Foundation held its 4th meeting of 
1995 on Tuesday, September 12th at NRSC Head- 
quarters on Capitol Hill. 


Thirty Foundation members and guests were briefed 
on the issues before the Senate Finance Committee by 
the Chief of Staff and General Counsel, the Inter- 
national Trade Counsel and the Tax Counsel. Follow- 
ing the briefings, Foundation members had the op- 
portunity for in-depth, personal discussions on the 
issues during a reception and working dinner with 
Senators D’Amato, Bond, Simpson, Chafee, Murkowski, 
Pressler, Grassley and Nickles, as well as the new 
Finance Committee Chairman, Senator Roth, and the 
Finance Committee staffers. We were very pleased to 
have fully eight of the ten Republican Senators on the 
Finance Committee in attendance, with Senator Roth in 
only his second day in the chairmanship. 


We would like to answer questions or listen to any 
ideas you might have regarding the above items or the

DECLARATION OF HERBERT E. ALEXANDER, Ph. D 


This report provides you with the information that 
you have requested regarding my opinions related to 
the case of Colorado Republican Federal Campaign 
Committee and Douglas Jones, as Treasurer, v. Fed- 
eral Election Commission. It also summarized my 
qualifications, and it provides you with information 
about my publications and one recent case in which I 
have rendered an expert opinion. 


PERSONAL BACKGROUND 


I am currently the director of the Citizens’ Research 
Foundation at the University of Southern California. I 
have held that position since 1958. CFR is devoted to 
bringing new awareness and understanding to the 
problems of political finance. It has pioneered the study 
of money in the political process: where it comes from, 
how it is handled and spent, and how it affects our 
democratic processes. It serves as a non -partisan 
observer and interpreter of trends in political finance 
and election reform, and it undertakes research, 
studies, seminars and publications to those ends. It also 
serves as an information clearinghouse for scholars, the 


media, and policymakers. 


In addition to serving as director of CRF, I am 
professor of political science at USC. As reflected in 
my curriculum vitae, a copy of which is attached as 
Exhibit A, my expertise lies in the area of political 
finance. I have studied, written, and taught on issues 
related to political finance for more than thirty years, 
and I have been recognized as one of the leading 
experts in the field. I have served on several com- 
missions (including the President’s Commission on

edited more than twenty books and 200 articles on 


matters relating to mo j iti .

MODERN CAMPAIGN FINANCE REFORM 


Few would argue that many of the political reforms 
that were set in motion more than half a century ago 
were no healthy for the American body politic. In 
retrospect, however, some of those reforms caused new 
problems. For example, in the interest of taking the 
selection of candidates out of the hands of political 
party bosses, the system of primary elections was 
inaugurated. That system today has swollen to gigantic 
proportions—primary costs are high, candidates are 
numerous, voter turnout is low, the parties are weaker 
because their nomination function has been lost-and 
primaries are far from being the kinds of referenda that 
reformers in the early 20th century envisioned. 


Like the earlier political reforms, those of the early 
1970s relating to political finance have brought both 
intended and unintended results. The wave of reform 
in the early 1970s was fundamentally an effort to 
improve a system perceived by many as fraught with 
favoritism and corruption. Many observers felt the 
American system of financing elections through often 
unlimited private donations gave undue influence in 
politics and government to wealthy or well-organized 
donors at the expense of the un-wealthy, the 
unorganized and the general public. Indeed, the system 
did have its share of secret money, unreported money, 
extorted money, corporate money, laundered money, 
foreign money, even tax-free money. The political 
financing reforms of the early 1970s sought to relocate 
political power, to reduce the perceived influence and 
power of monied interests and to extend and expand

citizen participation in the governing process. It is

campaigns, not by stifling political dialogue. Money is 
needed to amplify free speech so it can be heard by the 
electorate. Candidates need to convey their records, 
qualifications and programs in their own words, not as 
mediated by the media. 


While there are many voices today calling for 
lowered campaign costs and a level playing field, the 
fact remains that there are many sources of funding 
willing to contribute to candidates, political action 
committees and political party committees, and to 
spend money directly on independent expenditures, 
issue advertising, soft money and self-financed candi- 
dacy. A major difficulty is that some political activities 
are outside the scope of the FECA, and therefore, 
significantly, are not accountable. When campaign 
funds are raised and spent by political parties coor- 
dinating their activities with candidates, the ballot 
provides accountability: candidates can be voted out of 
office and political parties can be voted out of control. 


Money is not the sole campaign resource, but it is 
convertible into many of the resources candidates need 
to mount effective campaigns: professional staff, mass 
media advertising and sophisticated campaign technolo- 
gies. Money may not win elections—the candidate who 
spends the most is not always victorious—but a strong 
case may be made that an essential element of modern 


campaigns is adequate financing. 


One unintended result of campaign reforms has 
been to reinforce the splintering tendencies in our 
political process by limiting the roles political parties 
can play. Public funding directly to candidates has 
served to diminish the influence of the political parties 
because it helped free each candidate’s personal organ-

candidates and political parties and reduce the domi- 
nance of big money while opening opportunities for

we apply democratic principles to elections in an age of 
media politics, seemingly dominated by an atmosphere 
of dollar politics, in ways consonant with constitutional 
guarantees. The electoral process is a classic case of 
conflict between the democratic theory of full public 
dialogue in free elections and the conditions of an 


economic marketplace. 


Money, after all, is symbolic. The deeper competi- 
tion is for power or prestige or other values. In this 
sense, money is instrumental. Its importance is in the 
ways in which it is used by people to try to gain 
influence, or is converted into other resources, or used 
in combination with other resources, to achieve political 


power. 


But power is distributed unequally in society; it 
does not vary directly with wealth, status, skill, or any 
other single characteristic. Rather, degree of power is 
determined by many such factors, no one of which 
stands alone. And none of which has meaning unless 
related to the purposes of the individual and the 
environment in which one acts. So money is but one 
element in the equation of political power. But is the 
common denominator in the shaping of many of the 
factors comprising political power because it buys what 
is not or cannot be volunteered. Giving money permits 
numbers of citizens to share energy that must go into 
politics. Especially in affluent America, surely many 
individuals find it easier to show their support for a 
candidate or their loyalty to a party by writing a check 
than by devoting time to campaign or political work. Of

THE IMPORTACT OF PARTIES 


Today, there are more definable groups 
— seeking attention—business, labor, folate 
“ mt agent oa each has its subgroups dealing with 
peci Apes or issue. Larger grouping are 
—— — » 80 the voices heard most effectively are 
pace — resources or those achieving dramatic 
wpe 4 mony m may be more extensive than ever but 
1s a vast clamor. The fragmentation is 


114 


evident in independent voting, and few broadly-based 
groups can deliver voters who have diverse interests. 
Indeed, this situation is a partial cause of current public 
dissatisfaction, and prompts the need for restructured 
and improved political parties. But to accomplish this 
goal, parties need to be as free of legal restraints as is 
possible. No force other than political parties can 
produce coherent majorities working in the public 
interest. There is need for new incentives to encourage 
citizen participation in the electoral process through the 
parties, and an open environment in which public policy 
alternatives can be debated fully and freely within the 
context of a vibrant tow-party system. 


This line of reasoning suggests that it is desirable to 
give incentive for as much political activity as possible 
to be directed into regulated and disclosed channels, 
through candidates and parties particularly. In this 
regard, the roles of political parties are crucial, as 
regulated and disclosed channels of political activity, 
accountable to the electorate in the next election. 


Most political scientists believe that political parties 
play an essential role in developing, maintaining, and 
securing a strong and effective democracy in the United 
States. The late professor Clinton Rossiter, in Parties 
and Politics in America, wrote: “there is no America 
without democracy, no democracy without politics, and 
no politics without parties.’ Another eminent political

campaigns of their own, or even better, work with their 
candidates in joint campaigns. Now one of the last 
remaining ties between the party and the candidate- 
money-has been restricted or provided by government 
directly to some candidates, thus reinforcing the 
splintering and fragmenting tendencies already present 
in our candidate-centered culture. 


Party decline has led one political scientist, James 
David Barber, of Duke University to suggest that 
“(t]he media in the United States are the new political 


parties.” 


Political parties have adjusted to the advent of 
modern technology, professional campaign consultants, 
and candidate-centered campaigns by developing 
financial, technical and organizational resources of their 
own, enabling them to play significant roles in present- 
day campaigns. Parties are a multi-layered 
phenomenon, involving many different kinds of

PACs AND PARTIES: A COMPARISON 


It is inaccurate to characterize parties as “super 
PACs” or PACs as “mini-parties.”. PACs may some- 
times seek to influence party nominations, occasionally 
with their own candidates, but PACs generally do not 
extend their electoral activities beyond making 
financial contributions, seeking to influence candidate 
recruitment by parties, and voter mobilization. Many 
interest groups are unable to mobilize their supporters 
as readily as they once could, and their main activities 
are in financing and also in lobbying once candidates are 


elected to office. 


Political party committees generally do not become 
involved in financing the campaign of any candidate of 
the party’s nomination, unless that of an incumbent 
President or Governor running for reelection. PACs 
may contribute as much as $5,000 to any candidate for 
presidential nominations, but unlike individual 
contributions, PAC contributions are not matchable by 
federal funds. In practice, PACs have played a rather 
limited role in direct presidential pre-nomination 
campaign financing; in recent years, PACs have 
contributed less than 1 percent of the pre-nomination 
receipts of presidential candidates. 


In the general election period, majory party 
nominees for the presidency are automatically eligible 
for public treasury grants to finance their campaigns. 
These grants are given directly to the candidates’ own 
campaign organizations. The public grant also serves 
as a general election campaign expenditure limit. 
Minor party and new party candidates may qualify for 
partial public subsides under specified conditions. 
Major party nominees who accept public funding for the 


119 


general election period are prohibited from raising 
peer nee to further their campaign efforts

sources to help pay the costs of complying with the 
campaign finance laws. In recent years, presidential 
candidates and their staffs also have worked to rai 
soft money” for use by their party. _ aie

120 


states in which such activities are conducted. The state 
laws often are more permissive than federal laws; some 
twenty states permit corporate and labor union political 
contributions and give freer rein to individual and PAC 
contributions. Often, too, the state disclosure 
provisions are not as strick as the federal disclosure 


requirements. 


Party committees also may spend unlimited 
amounts on preparing and distributing sample ballots 
or slate cards and on campaign materials used in 
conjunction with grass-roots, volunteer campaign 
activity, such as buttons, yard signs and bumper 
stickers. Such spending may benefit the entire party 
ticket from the presidential and vice-presidential 
candidates to candidates for local offices. 


In a surprising development in 1996, large amounts 
of soft money, about $69 million, was spent by the 
parties on televised issue advertising that was 
nationally directed by the presidential campaigns; this 
became a very controversial use of what was intended 
by the 1979 Amendments to the Federal Election 
Campaign Act to be money for party grassroots 


activities. 


PACs may not contribute directly to a publicly- 
funded presidential general election campaign. Such 
committees and their corporate or labor organization 
sponsors, however, may spend treasury funds to assist 
specific presidential candidates, for example, through 
partisan communications with their restricted classes of 
persons, through ostensibly non-partisan voter regis- 
tration and turnout drives and, in the case of PACs, 
through independent expenditures, or as softmoney.

% Federal Election Commission, “PAC Activity Increases in 
| : i] 29 j

appearance thereof, so clearly coordinated expenditures 
could be increased substantially or unlimited without 
fear of corruption. One party may have more money to 
spend on coordinated expenditures, causing some 


grumbling, but neither major party has made official 
complaints about the factor. 


The political parties are limited in the amounts of 
coordinated expenditures they can make on behalf of 
both presidential and congressional candidates, but the 
limits were enacted with a view to reducing overall 
spending, not because coordinated expenditures were 
in any way considered to be corrupting. Coordinated 
expenditures could be without limit and there would be 
no question of corruption or the appearance of 
corruption. Individuals and PACs were not given the 
privilege of coordinated expenditures: they were 
limited in the amounts they could give, but beyond 
them, of course, they can engage in independent 
expenditures, issue advertising, or giving soft money. 
The reason the law specifies coordinated expenditures 
lays in the harmonious interactions that parties and 
most—but not all—candidates have. Political science 
studies have shown that party loyalty is a stronger 
determinant of roll call voting in the Senate and House 
than are ideology, constituent needs and desire, or 
patterns of contributions.” Thus coordinated expendi- 
tures are sanctioned by law but because they are 
limited by voting age population in any state, they tend 
to be minimal even in very large states. In Colorado,

& Company, 1984), pp. 136-40.

party coordinated expenditures on behalf of a senatorial 
candidate were only $170,932 in the 1996 elections. 


Historically, political parties at the peak of their 
influence, recruited candidates and, with the aid of 
patronage, jobs and other favors, enlisted armies of 
campaign workers who canvassed door-to-door, orga- 
nized meetings and rallies and turned out the vote. 
State and local committees were of chief importance in 
conducting this electoral activity, not only in senatorial 
and congressional elections but also in presidential 
elections. That party committees, particularly national 
party committees, can provide candidates with limited 
sums of campaign money—as now is the case at least 
with the party coordinated expenditures on behalf of 
presidential and selected congressional candidates— 
simply represents significant amounts of money in 
statutorily-sanctioned party activity on behalf of 
candidates. 


In terms of dollars, party support outweighs PAC 
support of presidential candidates whereas PAC 
contributions are greater in Senate and House 
campaigns. In the aggregate PACs can contribute 
more than parties because there is no aggregate limit 
for PACs; PACs can contribute up to the contribution 
limit to an unlimited number of candidates. Party 
support cannot be evaluated only in dollars but must 
include intangibles, such as the value of campaign 
related activities and the candidate’s use of the party 
label. Recognition of these facts helps place 
comparisons of PACs and parties in perspective. 


The United States has not developed labor parties 
or Christian Democratic parties, as other mature 
democracies have. The country is too diverse, with

126 


Additionally, PACs are a mechanism for political fund 
raising that respects the manner in which society is 
structured. Occupation and interest groups, which 
PACs represent, have replaced the neighborhood 
wards or precincts as centers of political activity. 


Thus PACs encourage the lively pluralism so highly 
valued and forcefully guaranteed by the Constitution. 
PACs also help ensure the transmission of candidates’ 
views and positions, while encouraging the 
participation of individuals without wealth to run for 
office. Finally, PACs had contributed to greater 
accountability in political financing. By sanctioning the 
use of PACs, the laws governing them have replaced 
the undisclosed and questionable forms of secret money 
derived from sources PACs now represent. But PACs 
are relatively new in the political arena compared with 
parties which have endured through American history. 


Political parties have some surface similarities with 
PACs and other groups: they all act in the political 
arena, they all seek to elect candidates of their choice, 
they all spend money, they all want some policy 
outcome. But parties have one vital difference from 
interest groups: while the various interest groups (and 
their PACs) usually have one specific goal or concern, 
political parties represent an amalgam or coalition of 
interests and goals; moreover, the purpose of parties is 
to gain control of government, rather than to pursue 
single goals, as PACs do. For example, the Sierra Club 
seeks to protect the environment. The Business 
Roundtable looks out for big business. The AFL-CIO 
represents union members and workers. The NAACP 
speaks out for racial minorities. The National

—_ omen pushes feminist causes. And

unifying force in this system, building coalitions and

Limits on party contributions or expenditures 
cannot be considered merely marginal restrictions. The 
primary purpose is for the party to communicate as 
widely as is possible, and it does this in part on its own 
and in part through its candidates. In lieu of unlimited 
party support, PACs representing special interests 
compete for the candidates’ attention. It enhances 
democracy to encourage the broader interests parties 
represent, than the narrower interests of PACs. Party 
support leads to enhanced accountability, and hence is 


highly desirable. 


CORRUPTION 


The word “corruption” is widely misused and wildly 
exaggerated. Some but not all of what is called 
corruption consists of legally-sanctioned actions that 
some people do not like; political contributions when 
defined and limited in iaw would seem to fall in that 
category. Political corruption cannot be defined in the 
commonly-accepted meaning of the term. It is not 
bribery, nor even legalized bribery, as some critics put 
it: that is covered by existing statutes relating to one- 
on-one exchanges of money. Rather, in political terms, 
the giving of a sizable contribution usually represents 
only a potential of good will and perhaps disproportion- 
ate access to public official in order to plead a case for 
public policy change. It is a means of gaining access in 
order to make one’s view known. While it may ensure 
access and perhaps unequal political influence, it does 
not necessarily provide the claimant with a favorable 
public policy. If by corruption critics mean influence or 

influence-peddling, then these lead to a slippery slope

risk of actual corruption or the appearance of

Court’s landmark Buckley v. Valeo decision: “the 
primary interest served by the Act as a whole, is the 
prevention of corruption and the appearance of corrup- 
tion spawned by the real or imagined coercive influence 
of large financial contributions on candidates’ positions 
and on their actions if elected to office.” Thus, unless a 
party’s financial support of its candidates is somehow 
corruptive, or appears to be corruptive, the govern- 
ment has no compelling interest in limiting such 
activity. 

The Committee then wrote: “Political party support 
of its candidates is not corrupting. No conventional 
concept of corruption delineates the candidate-party 
relationship,” which needs to be understood as 
symbiotic.” After all, parties are formed to win elec- 
tions, not make a profit as in the political science defini- 
tion given above. Parties do not bribe candidates or 
“induce them to do wrong” as in the Webster’s defini- 
tion. (Private interests occasionally do as in the 
Abscam or “Keating Five” cases). 


A strong two-party system imbued with a profes- 
sional code of ethics might have prevented Watergate 
or the 1996 experience of alleged improprieties with 
respect to the solicitation and acceptance of certain 
foreign or other political contributions. The two major

The attitudes of Members of Congress toward their 
parties surely differ, but a common view was expressed 
by former Democratic House Speaker Thomas P. 
O'Neill. who stated that those who entered the Con- 
gress since Watergate and the Vietnam War” . . . had 
no loyalty to the party whatsoever. They looked down 
on it. They said, “The party didn’t elect me, and I’m not 
beholden to the party.” Campaign consultant David 
Garth even notes that “the [political] boss is a plus to 


have against you.”” 


While surely this O’Neill quotation is an exaggera- 
tion, some sixteen years later, clearly many Members 
are quite independent of the party, both programmati- 
cally and financially. Reinforcing that position, O'Neill 
also said that the party leadership in the House always 
understood and accepted that some Members had to 
keep their distance from the party.” In these cir- 
cumstances, the party would hardly be able to corrupt

2) Marti

154 


<r,iaterial.”” Purposive behavior includes those who 


participate in politics to influence governmental policies 
or to make a difference in the outcome of an election. 
Solidary includes those who give out of friendship for a 
candidate, those who enjoy the social contacts that 
politics provide, or those who seek recognition. 
Material behavior refers to those who give for business 
or employment reasons or because it is expected of 
someone in their position. These categories are not 
mutually exclusive and may jointly apply to motivations 
for giving by some individuals or PACs. 


Not all these categories apply to contributions made 
by party committees to candidates on the ticket. The 
most relevant is the purposive motivation, to seek to 
influence governmental policies and to make a differ- 
ence in the outcome of an election. This purpose well 
describes the ideological content of a party committee, 
which is surely to elect as many candidates as possible 
in order to gain control of a legislative chamber or an 
executive position, in order to further party piatforms 
and positions, as well as to benefit from the spoils of 
victory, the appointments and domination of the 


decision-making processes of government. 


This review of relationships among parties and pub- 
lic officials leads to the conclusion that party spending 
does not lead to corruption, nor would unlimited party 


assistance to candidates lead to corruption.

INDEPENDENT EXPENDITURES 


Independent expenditures are much more likely to 
help or hurt individual candidates and only indirectly to 
affect parties. One exception occurred in 1980, when 
independent advertising against five Democratic Sena- 
tors was effective in helping Republicans gain control of 


the U.S. Senate. 


In upholding the First Amendment guarantees of 
freedom of speech and freedom of association, courts 
have consistently held that individuals and groups have 
a right to use independent expenditures to communi- 
cate with voters; while the law may limit expenditures 
by a campaign in circumstances where public financing 
is provided in exchange, individuals and groups have 
the right to spend unlimited amounts of money to 
advance or undermine a given candidacy, so long as 
they do not coordinate their activities with the cam- 
paign. 

While supporting the letter and spirit of this 
doctrine, nevertheless, independent expenditures raise 
questions about accountability; independent expendi- 
tures can shape campaign discourse and popular opinion 
but, unlike candidates and parties, individuals and 
groups engaging in independent expenditures do not 

appear on the ballot and, as a result, cannot be elec- 
torally rewarded or punished for their conduct. Thus, 
despite their disclosure, independent expenditures lack 


137 


accountability and t 
pac Sa hey are an inefficient way to

Independent expenditures are not natural acts for 


parties. 


The normal workings of parties include: 


138 


recruiting candidates and writing platforms, raising 
funds to support those and candidates and organizing 
voters. It seems to be an unnatural act for a party to go 
outside its normal structure with an “independent 
expenditure.” Yet, if the campaign finance reformers 
succeed and outlaw these natural acts for the parties, 
then they will increasingly have to use independent 
expenditures to get their message out. Candidates 
cannot win without a clearly communicated message 
that is heard by a majority of the voters. If parties 
cannot present their message through the traditional 
means—i.e., advertising—then the burden will fall on 
other groups, mainly special interests. What makes the 
most sense is to give the widest berth to permit parties 
to participate as fully as they can; preventing parties 
from electioneering will only leave message pre- 
sentation up to uncontrollable and unaccountable 


special interests. 


On its face, the theory of independent expenditures 
conflicts with the idea of coordinated expenditures. 
One may ask how a part can both communicate with 
candidates in providing money support, while at the 
same time not communicate when providing indepen- 
dent expenditures on behalf of its candidate or against 
its candidate’s opponent. Certainly unlimited coordi- 
nated expenditures are a preferred way to proceed. 


SPENDING LIMITS AND FREE SPEECH 


One aspect of free speech is the question of whether 
to impose spending limits. Those who advocate 
expenditure limit do so on two grounds: (1) to control 
high costs; and (2) to level the playing field so there is 
not a wide disparity between spending by candidates 
for the same office. In response, it can be said that

Adding together the amounts spent a at 
aspects of the presidential campaigns in 1996, 
Echibit C, a total of $151.4 million was spent by or on 


a 141 


behalf of the Clinton-Gore ticket, and $130.3 million by 
or on behalf of the Dole-Kemp ticket. 


Thus all three phases of the presidential selection 


process provided evidence that the overall spending 
limits were not effective. 


While there was increased spending by the parties 
and candidates in 1996, there also was a marked 
increase in spending on behalf of, or against, candidates 
by political parties, labor unions, businesses, the 
Christian Coalition and others. Some of this money was 
disclosed and some was not disclosed, and some was 
beyond the control of the candidates and parties. In 
contrast, party spending is fully disclosed, and the 


parties and candidates jointly control how the money is 
spent. 


Indeed, the payouts from the Presidential Election 
Campaign Fund hit an all-time high of $234 million in 
1996, and the Citizens’ Research Foundation estimate 
that a total of 4 billion dollars was spent on all political 
campaigning in the 1995-96 election cycle. (For 
perspective that figure is roughly equal to the federal 
deficit in 1970, but a bit less than the combined 
advertising budgets in 1996 of the country’s two largest 
advertisers, Procter and Gamble and Philip Morris). 


At the very least, the development of these three 
parallel campaigns underlines the futility of attempting 
to impose a strict system of campaign spending limits. 
In our political ‘ystem-animated by a variety of 
competing interests, each guaranteed freedom of 
&xpression—when the flow of money is restricted at 
any point in the campaign process, it will inexorably 
carve new channels through which individuals and 


142 


groups can seek to influence political campaigns and 
elections. 


Our pluralistic system has been criticized as 
favoring organized groups with abundant memberships 
or resources, but not necessarily helping the unorgan- 
ized: the poor, the black, consumers, youth and the 
elderly. One of the roles of political parties is to advo- 
cate public policies that would assist these elements of 
the population. Indeed, parties are sometimes charged 
with being servants of special interests but they are as 
often countervailing forces against certain special 
interests. They cannot long be identified solely with a 
single special interest to the detriment of their wider 
role as aggregators or brokers of numerous, sometimes 


opposed, diverse interests. 


CONCLUSION: POLITICAL REFORM AND REALIS- 
TIC IDEALISM 


Politics is about people and groups of people, their 
ideas, interests and aspirations. Since people seek 
political fulfillment partly through parties and partly 
through interest groups, a politics in which supportive 
groups are excluded or seriously impaired is difficult to 
envisage. Too many ideas and interests of value to 
society would get lost without the organized participa- 
tion of groups in electoral politics. Some groups with 
few members participate mainly through their wealth. 
Since people and groups differ, conflict occurs, but it 
does so in a political arena in which government sets 
the rules and the players are expected to play by them. 
The government, however, is also a player and the only 
fail-safe guarantee against its dominance lies in the 
ability of groups and interests in society to articulate 
their demands, to coalesce through political parties, and 


143 


to Oppose government polici ’

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT FOR 
THE DISTRICT OF COLORADO 


Civil Action No. 89-N-1159 
FEDERAL ELECTION COMMISSION, PLAINTIFF 


v. 


COLORADO REPUBLICAN FEDERAL CAMPAIGN 
COMMITTEE, ET AL., DEFENDANTS 


| Filed: ; 


AFFIDAVIT OF DONALD K. BAIN 


DONALD K. BAIN, the affiant , being first duly sworn, 
deposes and says : 

1. My name is Donald K. Bain. | am chairman of the 
Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Commies 
(Party), and have held that position since March, 1993. 
I have been involved in Colorado politics since the 
late1950’s and have held numerous positions from block 
worker to state party chairman. I am of legal age and 
competent to testify. 


2 Ihave read the First Amended Counterclaim that 


the Party intends to file in its lawsuit against the FEC 
and I verify the factual statements made therein. 


159 


3. In particular, I attest that the Party was, is, and 
for the foreseeable future will be ready, willing, and 
able to spend more in working with our candidates to 
support their candidacies than the pennies per voter 
allowed by the Federal Election Campaign Act 
(FECA). However, although we believe that the 
FECA limits on our coordinated spending are invalid, 
the Party is reluctant to exceed them and incur the risk 
of sanctions. Thus, we have been forced to forego 
political speech and association with our candidates that 
we believe to be constitutionally protected. 


4. At one point during the 1996 campaign, a coordi- 
nated effort with one of our candidates seemed to be so 
necessary that we seriously considered the possibility 
of spending in excess of the FECA limits, reporting the 
expenditures (as we always do), and defending any 
FEC action on the grounds that the limits violate the 
First Amendment. Our candidate advised, however, 
that he was unwilling to cooperate in campaign activity 
that the FEC was certain to challenge. Accordingly, 
because of the FECA limits, the Party refrained from 
political speech that we considered urgently important. 


5. The ability to make independent expenditures in 
support of our candidates, while welcome, is no sub- 
stitute for the right to coordinate our political speech 
with our candidates and disseminate efficiently our 
common messages. We can be far more efficient, and 
our communications can be more focused, under- 
standable, and effective if the Party and its candidates 
can work together. 


6. Moreover, uncertainty as to what the FEC will 
regard as coordinated and the difficulties and burdens 
of refuting charges of coordination interfere with the 


160 


internal affairs of the Party. We exist in large part to 
support our candidates. We need to understand what 
help our candidates need and to assist in developing 
programs to best communicate our common views. It is 
very awkward for us, at the same time, to be worrying 
about how to preserve and prove our independence 
with respect to programs we pursue in support of our 
candidates. Inevitably, both some otherwise permissi- 
ble independent speech and some coordinated speech 
are chilled and avoided. 


7. My reference to difficulties of proof is deliberate. 
Complaints to the FEC have become commonplace in 
federal campaigns, and they can be damaging, both 
politicatly and financially. They must be defended, but 
the cost of proving you are right can, in itself, be 
serious punishment. As a result, whenever legal stan- 
dards or factual situations are unclear, the Party has a 
strong incentive to steer clear and much permissible 
speech is deterred. 


8. My experience has been that political campaigns 
are fluid and fast paced. Any rules must be clear and 
simple and depend on easily established objective facts. 
Otherwise, the result will be either self censorship, as 
the Party and its candidates avoid potential contro- 
versy at the expense of foregoing lawful but ambiguous 
activity, or extensive and burdensome litigation that 
eats up Party resources intended for political associa- 
tion and speech, not for lawyers. 


I swear under penalty of perjury that the foregoing is 
true and accurate to the best of my knowledge and 
belief.

November, 1996. 


/s/ DONALD K. BAIN
