IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF COLORADO 
JUDGE EDWARD W. NOTTINGHAM 


Civil Action No. 89 N 1159 


FEDERAL ELEctTi IN COMMISSION. 
PLAINTIFF/COUNTER-] IEFENDANT 


;* 
COLORADO REPURLI ‘AN FEDERAL CAMPAIGN 


COMMITTE E, ET AL., DEFENDA NTS/COUNTER- 
PLAINTIFFS 


(Filed: Apr. 21, 1997) 


Declaration of Leon G. Billings 


l. My name is Leon G. Billings. From March 1982 
until mid 1983 I was the Executive Director of the 
Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee (DSCC). 
Sen. Wendell Ford was the Chairman of the DSCC at 
that time, and Sen. Alan Cranston chaired the Senate 
Democratic Leadership Circle (DLC), which was the 
major fundraising arm of the DSCC. 


164 


» The DLC was created after the Democrats lost 
the Senate in 1980. Sen. Cranston controlled the 
fundraising for the DLC, and he had a separate staff 
person running that operation. However, the DLC did 
not determine how the money was spent. That was left 


to the DSCC. 


2 The DSCC had a three-Member Executive 
Committee comprised of Sen. Ford. Sen. Cranston, and 
Sen. Bill Bradley. They basically made the decisions as 
to how to distribute the money, although there was also 
a larger committee that met occasionally to provide 
direction. 


4. The DLC Leadership Circle bank account was 
a separate account. Funds would be transferred from 
that account to the DSCC account, which then made the 
funds available for the candidates. 


5. To the best of my recollection, in 1982, the 
DSCC had $1.2 million to distribute. That seems like a 
paltry sum today, but there were a number of por 
competitive races where the DSC C was able to make 
the maximum coordinated expenditure permitted under 
2 U.S.C. § 441a(d). I think this was around $67,000. We 
were not able to spend the maximum amount in 
California, for example, where the limit was $500,000. 
We tended to focus our resources on Senators In tighter 
races, but another decisive factor was the extent to 
which incumbents helped raise money for the DSC Cc. 


6. Sen. Cranston was far and eway the best 
fundraiser the Democrats had. He had absolutely no 
reservations about asking for money. Sen. Cranston 
alsc enlisted the assistance of other key incumbent 
Senators who were in a position to raise money. By and 


165 


large he focused on Senators running for reelection in 
that cycle. Other Senators had different styles, and 
used different approaches to fundraising. 


7. My understanding was that Sens. Kennedy, 
Jackson, and Metzenbaum all had unwritten 
commitments from the DSCC that they would get back 
as much as they raised as a way to encourage Senators 
to raise money for the party. 


8. Senators solicited a coterie of rich Democrats 
who traditionally have provided much of the Party’s 
support. The DLC membership was $15,000, so one 
hundred DLC members would contribute $1.5 million. 
Sen. Cranston’s goal was to have two DLC members in 
every State, and he came very close to that goal. He 
kept a list of contributors and would keep after 


Senators who had not yet recruited two members from 
their states. 


¥. The DLC sponsored elaborate events and 
forums where DLC members could socialize with other 
contributors and party leaders. Even though $15,000 
does not seem like much today, this was enough to gain 
admission to these exclusive events in 1982. 


10. The Senators who solicited the money for the 
DSCC could not promise that the money would be used 
directly in support of their own or another particular 
campaign, because earmarking contributions was 
prohibited. However, that did not mean that Senators 
who raised money could not be assured that they would 
get a significant return from their efforts. There 
appeared to be an understanding between the DSCC 
and the Senators that the amount of money they 


166 


received from the DSCC was related to how much they 
raised for the Committee. 


11. The DSCC also raised money on its own behalf. 
Sen. Ford and I took whirlwind trips to relatively small 
fundraising events, in the $15,000-$25,000 range. We 
also hosted events at the Democratic Party’s mid-term 
Convention; and the DSCC started a direct mail cam- 
paign during my time as Executive Director. 


12. People often contribute to party committees 
because they have given the maximum amount to a 
candidate, and want to help the candidate indirectly by 
contributing to the party. The DSCC was able to 
provide substantial indirect support to Senatorial cam- 
paigns. For example, we did polling, and made the 
results available to the campaigns. We also made in 
kind contributions under the § 441la(d) limits. The 
DSCC made the decision as to how much a particular 
campaign was going to get, but the individual 
campaigns decided how it would be used. 


13. In my view, contributions help contributors 
gain access, have their phone calls taken. And access 
can influence action, since the totality of the infor- 
mation a Member has is significantly prejudiced by the 
people who have access. A Member can feel guilty 
about taking money from those whose positions they 
rarely support. 


14. I never called a Senator’s office on behalf of a 
contributor’s position. However, I once had a contri- 
butor ask me to set up an appointment with a Senator. 
I did pave the way by calling the Senator’s Admini- 
strative Assistant to say, if it was convenient, this 
person would be calling for an appointment. When the 


167 


contributor called again with the same request, I 
turned him down. 


15. There has to be some way of controlling 
distribution of the time available to talk to people, and 
priorities are more likely to be given both to calls where 
a Senator is soliciting money, or is talking to those who 
have contributed. That is what is wrong with the 
system today: the amount of time Senators spend talk- 
ing to people they want to contribute, and talking to 


people who have already contributed, seeking addi- 
tional contributions. 


16. The DSCC and the state party committees 
have Separate coordinated expenditure limits under 
§ 44la(d). My recollection is that we sent a letter to 
each state party committee saying we planned to spend 
their share, and they should notify us if that was not 
acceptable. I think one state party resisted until it 
became clear to them that if they did not authorize the 
DSCC to spend their share, the state party would have 
to raise and spend this amount. Once the state party 
understood this, they agreed to let the DSCC spend the 
state share. I believe we had the Democratic National 


Committee (DNC) transfer their § 441a(d) authority to 
us, as well. 


VW I started a candidate retreat, at which cam- 
paign strategists, pollsters, media experts and election 
analysts provided a two-day intensive course on 
fundraising, campaigning, selecting consultants and so 
forth. We had lists of Democratic political consultants 
and pollsters they might consider, and we also warned 


campaigns not to hire those who played both sides of 
the aisle. 


168 


18. While we welcomed calls from all our candi- 
dates, we spent nearly all our time working with non- 
incumbents. Incumbents rarely sought our advice, and 
often rejected it when we offered. 


19. Taking away the limits on coordinated expendi- 
tures would result in a fundamental transferal of power 
to certain individual Senators. In that situation, it 
would behoove the leadership to raise and control as 
much money as possible. 


20. As I understand it, the national campaign 
committees now send competent, qualified campaign 
coordinators into the various States to help candidates, 
particularly non-incumbents. If the limits on coordi- 
nated expenditures are removed, a party committee 
would be able tell a candidate, “We are going to give 
you $2 million or $4 million for your Senate race. We 
are also going to tell you who is going to staff your 
Senate race. We are going to tell you what to say and 
what to do. We are going to take control.” This would 
have enormous political and policy implications. 


21. Most Senators and Senate candidates have a 
political philosophy that is unlikely to change dramati- 
cally. However, their views on some issues may not be 
as well established, so this is where money can have a 
major impact. Contacts from contributors can 
determine whether a Senator takes an active role on a 
particular issue, or declines to take an active role. 


22. This was readily apparent to me in my work in 
the 1970s, when I worked with Sen. Edmund Muskie on 
environmental legislation. When working with 400 or 
2,000 page bills, it was commonplace for Members to try 


169 


to make what seem to be minor changes that in fact 
benefit their natural constituency enormously. 


23. To the extent that this is corruption, it has 
always been part of our process. Daniel Webster used 
to sell votes. This is not going to change. What is going 
to change, if you have unlimited party expenditures, is 
that party leaders will be able to say who gets 
nominated and who gets elected. While I am not sure 
that the party leaders who control this process would 
be more beholden to their contributors than is now the 


case, their power to make things happen would 
increase. 


24. The argument I have always made in favor of 
Political Action Committees is that you diversify the 
corruption. Is it better to have a Senator responding to 
400 different political action committees with diverse 
political interests’ Or to have one party committee 
responding only to the party leadership? Tt scares me 
to think that you can go out and find a lackey and tell 
that lackey you are going to give him or her $4 million, 
which he or she will use to defeat a much better candi- 
date who is still running an old fashioned campaign, and 
will not accept a $4 million contribution. This would be 
a very different political environment. with less 
representation of constituents than is now the case. 


25. Pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1746, I declare under 
penalty of perjury that the foregoing is true and 
correct, 


/s/ LEON G. BILLINGS 
LEON G. BILLINGS 


Executed on this 15 day of April, 1997.

173 


Statement of Anthony Corrado 
Associate Professor of Government, Colby College 


Political parties are essential to the vitality of 
American democracy. They serve a unique and crucial 
role in our political system, a role so important that 
virtually all political scientists and most learned 
observers would agree with the conclusion drawn over 
a half-century ago by the noted scholar E. E. Schat- 
tschneider that “modern democracy is unthinkable save 
in terms of the parties” (1942: 1). Party organizations 
serve as the principal intermediaries between the citi- 
zenry and government. They organize large numbers 
of individuals and political groups into collectives of 
active political participants, and combine the diverse 
and diffuse views of individual members into broad- 
based expressions of political speech. In doing so, they 
help to translate the needs and desires of the general 
public into political positions and public policies that 
form the basis of civic debate and government action. 


Parties seek to communicate their views and coalesce 
diverse interests behind policy positions predominantly 
through electoral activity. Party organizations con- 
centrate a substantial share of their resources and 
activities on political campaigning. They play a central 
role in recruiting candidates, raising campaign funds, 
communicating information to voters, and encouraging 
citizens to vote. They develop messages and platforms 
designed to appeal to broad-based majorities in an 
effort to win elections and build legislative majorities. 
These messages provide voters with outlooks that help 
them comprehend the choices available and structure 
their decisionmaking. In particular, the parties provide 


174 


citizens, especially those who identify themselves as 
party supporters, with perceptual frameworks and 
voting cues that help them assess candidates and 


Issues. 


Traditionally, parties have also assumed the major 
responsibility for raising the monies needed to finance 
election-related activities and candidate campaigns. 
For most of our history, campaign financing was essen- 
tially party financing, since party organizations were 
responsible for nominating candidates at all levels of 
government and conducting campaigns on their behalf. 
For the most part, party committees raised the money 
and paid for election-related expenses; candidates did 
not establish their own private campaign organizations 
or run separate financial operations. 


This system of campaign financing changed dramati- 
cally in the decades after World War IL. The wide- 
spread growth of individual candidate campaign orga- 
nizations and the consequent decline in the role of party 
organizations led to the development of candidate- 
based politics in which individual candidates raised 
their own campaign monies. Party organizations thus 
became secondary institutions in political finance. 
While they continued to provide direct assistance to 
candidates, they were more important as financiers of 
general party communications and issues development, 
as well as such crucial party-building and voter par- 
ticipation efforts as grass-roots organizational activity 
and voter turnout programs. 


By the tame Congress adopted the Federal Election 
Campaign Act (FECA) in 1974, the vast majority of 
political scientists and many journalists were already 
warning of the need to strengthen political parties. But 


175 


the Congress was more concerned with controlling 
rising campaign costs and equalizing the resources 
available to candidates. It is also a body of incumbents, 
who were more interested in establishing methods of 
candidate-based financing that have benefitted incum- 
bents. As a result, the FECA established a regulatory 
approach that further reduced the potential influence of 
party organizations. The law limited party contribu- 
tions to federal candidates, and placed severe restric- 
tions on the amounts they could spend in coordination 
with these candidates. It also prohibited party com- 
mittees from spending money independently on behalf 
of federal candidates. It therefore reduced the ability 
of party organizations to finance candidate-related 
activity, and constrained the role of these committees in 
communicating directly with the electorate. 


In this statement I will examine the role of party 
funding in the political system, especially the issues 
raised by the FECA limits on party expenditures. As 
noted by the Supreme Court in its recent decision in 
Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. 
Federal Election Commission, the regulations cur- 
rently imposed on party financial activity raise 
important First Amendment issues regarding political 
speech. The rules also raise a number of practical or 
policy concerns with respect to the ability of political 
parties to share their views effectively with the 
electorate and assist party members in their campaigns 
for elective office. This statement seeks to address 
these issues by reviewing the characteristics of party 
organizations and the forms of political party finance, 
the purpose and role of party expenditures, the ques- 
tion of whether party expenditures can be corruptive of 


176 


the political process, and the efficacy of regulatory 
alternatives with respect to party political speech. 


I. The Characteristics of Political Parties 
A. Political Parties and Their Roles: An Overview 


Political parties are unique institutions in American 
politics. They are best conceptualized as organized 
groups with broad-based public memberships that 
consist of three interrelated social structures: (1) the 
party organization, which consists of the formal and 
informal leaders and activists who eperate largely 
through formal party structures, ranging from the 
national party committees to local precinct committees; 
(2) the party in government, which consists of the 
elected and appointed officials who seek government 
office under the party label, and once in government 
articulate and work to advance party goals and posi- 
tions; and (3) the party in the electorate, which consists 


of those citizens who demonstrate some degree of 


loyalty to a party, agree with its philosophy and goals, 
tend to vote habitually for party candidates, and con- 
stitute the greater part of a party’s political coalition. 


This general description of political parties reflects 
the different purposes parties serve in democratic 
governance. The chief function parties perform is to 
contest elections. While other organized interests, such 
as political action committees and labor unions, support 
candidates, raise funds, broadcast advertisements, and 
work to turn out the vote, no other organization plays 
so central a role in our electoral system as the political 
party. The preeminent purpose of any party organiza- 
tion is to win elections. To this end, parties run can- 
didates for office, develop campaign messages, raise 


177 


funds, conduct polls, provide technical assistance to 
candidate campaigns, recruit grass-roots volunteers, 
and work to target and identify party voters and turn 
them out to vote for party candidates. Their goal in 
winning elections is to build legislative majorities and 
help to organize government so that voter preferences 


can be translated more effectively into government 
action. 


In order to successfully contest elections, parties 
must appeal to a broad range of interests in society. 
Given the size of the electorate and the large legislative 
districts that characterize federal and statewide elec- 
ions, parties can attract the support needed to win only 
by building extensive coalitions of diverse interests. 
Parties therefore unify a wide array of individuals and 
interest groups in a meaningful way. They work to 
resolve differences between organized interests or 
large blocs of voters, and to advance compromise posi- 
tions that will benefit the party’s common goals. 
Parties thus serve as a unifying force in the pluralistic 
and at times fragmented civil society in which we live. 


The primary way parties generate support is by 
promoting political discussion and encouraging the 
development of majority views on public policy issues. 
In their effort to attract supporters, party organiza- 
tions express their positions on major policy issues and 
develop platforms. These platforms often set forth 
broad agendas for future government action, such as 
the Democrats’ “Great Society” or the Republicans’ 
recent “Contract with America.” These party views 
clarify the choices voters face and help voters make 
‘informed decisions. They provide party candidates 
with common themes and policies that can serve as the 


178 


basis for their election campaigns. They serve as the 
basis for the party’s legislative priorities in govern- 
ment. And they help to frame political thinking in a 
way that makes the ensuing civic debate more 
accessible and understandable to party supporters. 


This role of parties offers a futher benefit: it en- 
hances the accountability and responsibility of the 
political system. By providing voters with clear 
choices, parties and their candidates assume a collective 
obligation to fulfill their commitments to their support- 
ers. Citizens can hold party leaders and officeholders 
accountable for party views; they know whom to praise 
or blame for any related government actions. If the 
party leaders fail to pursue the policies they advocate, 
they risk losing political support. Parties therefore 
have a strong incentive to uphold their responsibilities 
to the electorate. 


Parties also serve an important socialization role. 
That is, they educate citizens about the political process 
and policy issues, and encourage individuals to partici- 
pate in the process. Parties advocate political views 
that voters, or future voters, may adopt in developing 
their own individual political values and preferences. 
They thus constitute a means by which individuals can 
come to understand political activity, comprehend the 
meaning of political symbols and issue controversies, 
and learn the value of political action. Party organiza- 
tions also promote various forms of participation: they 
give individuals an effective and efficient way to join 
with others for political action, to contribute time and 
resources to political campaigns, and otherwise become 
involved in shared exercises in political speech and 
electioneering. 


179 


Finally, parties help to unify our highly fragmented 
system of government. Political power is divided 
between federal, state, and local levels of authority, anc 
is further subdivided by the separation of powers 
within each level of government, as well as by the 
diverse constituencies governments seek to serve. Par- 
ties work to overcome this decentralized distribution of 
authority by relying on shared symbols, traditions, and 
policy positions to create common bonds and perspec- 
tives that are shared by party members and elected 
officials at all levels of government. This common 
framework generates centripetal force in the political 
system, which, while not always successful, can produce 
the cohesion of interests and the legislative majorities 
that are needed for government to fulfill its purposes. 
Parties therefore are an essential means of making 
government more effective. 


B. Distinguishing Parties from Other Organized 
Groups 


Political parties are not unique in their desire to 
mobilize supporters to influence election outcomes or 
the behavior of those who hold public office. Indeed, 
most organized groups, especially the larger national 
political action committees (PACs) or institutions such 
as corporations or labor unions, have similar objectives 
with respect to their political activities. In recent 
years, large PACs and interest groups such as the 
AFL-CIO have engaged in many of the electoral activi- 
ties that have traditionally been associated with party 
committees. For example, PACs and other organized 
interests raise political money, make contributions to 
candidates, sponsor independent advertisements that 
seek to influence election outcomes, and work to 


180 


mobilize supporters and encourage them to vote. But 
despite these common activities, parties remain sub- 
stantially different from other political groups, and 
these differences must be respected. 


Although organized interest groups and political 
parties are both engaged in election-related activity, 
they do not pursue the same objectives in electoral 
politics. While the general purposes of these disparate 
types of organizations may coincide and even be com- 
parable at times, a fundamental distinction remains. As 
one scholar of party organizations has noted: 


Parties run candidates for office and seek control in 
order to organize the governing process. The 
parties stand for certain principles, but they use 
these principles chiefly as a means to attract 
adherents. Interest groups, contrarily, became 
involved in the electoral arena only to forward their 
policy preferences. Put simply, rather than taking 
positions to attract followers, they support 
candidates who support their positions—or oppose 
candidates who do not favor their positions. (Maisel, 
1987:93, emphasis in original) 


Accordingly, most PACs and interest groups exhibit 
bipartisan behavior. Since they are most concerned 
with advancing their narrow interest, they provide 
support to candidates who share their views, regardless 
of party affiliation. They also tend to favor incumbent 
legislators over challengers, since incumbents are 
already in a position to help them with their particular 
cause. 


While parties represent broad interests and diverse 
coalitions, PACs and other organized political groups 


181 


usually represent a single interest or a narrow range of 
interests. These groups exist primarily to advance a 
particular view or single legislative goal; their mission 
is primarily to encourage some government action that 
will provide the group and its members with a specific 
economic or other material benefit. In the pursuit of 
their specific interest, they use a variety of means 
ranging from public demonstrations and collective bar- 
gaining to candidate contributions and direct lobbying 
of Congress. 


Traditionally, these non-party groups have largely 
relied on their lobbying of legislators and, to a lesser 
extent, their lobbying of administration officials to 
achieve their ends. Only after their lobbying role was 
firmly established did these groups become involved in 
the electoral process in a meaningful way. Yet, even 
today, electoral campaigning remains only one of their 
activities, while, it is still the primary activity of party 
organizations. An organized political group thus differs 
from a party in that it maintains some sphere of 
nonpolitical action and turns to political or electoral 
action “when political avenues appear to be the best or 


the only means to achieve its goals” (Beck and Sorauf, 
1992:19). 


These differences between organized interests and 
party organizations are especially pronounced in regard 
to corporations and labor unions. These institutions 
exist for the economic benefit of their members; their 
major goal is to help their members materially and 
economically. Moreover, they have directly benefited 
from provisions of federal and state law that grant 
these institutions special privileges and protections, 
which provide them with economic advantages and are 


182 


essential to their ability to amass large aggregations of 
wealth. Consequently, for most of this century, 
corporations and labor unions have not been allowed to 
use their treasury funds in connection with federal ¢lec- 
tion activities, nor may they make direct contributions 
to federal candidates. Such participation in federal 
elections is prohibited, since it raises the possibility 
that these organizations could leverage their economic 
success in the marketplace into political influence that 
could be used to secure quid pro quos from legislators 
or otherwise unduly influence government action, In 
contrast, party organizations do not own independent 
economic enterprises or seek to generate wealth from 
commercial activities. In fact, federal regulations spe- 
cifically prohibit parties from using commercial means 
to finance their political activities (11 CFR $100.7 a}), 


Another important difference between parties and 
other political groups is that parties play a fundamental 
role in the organization of elections. Most states include 
party signs or symbols on their ballots and arrange 
ballots based on party affiliation. State laws often give 
party members a voice in the selection of their party's 
candidate in direct primaries. In many states, party 
members also help select local party leaders, such as 
precinct workers or ward chairs. Party members are 
therefore more than a group of citizens to be recruited 
and mobilized on Election Day; they have a recognized 
status under law that is not shared by other organized 
political groups. 


Finally, for a majority of the electorate, parties serve 
as important voting cues or symbols. The party 
affiliation of a candidate is the chief cue on which voters 
rely when making decisions about candidates or issues, 


183 


In this way, parties play a meaningful role in helping 
voters relate their own values and goals to the options 
offered in each election. No other organized group in 
American polities has so effective or extensive an influ- 
ence in helping to structure electoral decisionmaking. 
This distinction is in large part a result of the historic 
and enduring role of political parties. Unlike many 
PACs or political groups, the major parties have 
exhibited uncommon stability and endurance. Parties 
therefore serve as reliable reference points that have 
stood the test of time, and thus provide form and 
continuity in the national issue debates and the choices 
citizens face with each new election. 


II. National Political Party Finance 


Federal campaign finance laws cover Various sources 
of contributions and different types of spending. In 
keneral, the law places limits on the amounts indivi- 
duals, PACs, political committees, and party commit- 
lees can contribute to federal candidates, and prohibits 
corporations and labor unions from giving to federal 
candidates altogether. The law, however, does not limit 
most forms of political spending. Candidates can spend 
unlimited amounts from their personal wealth, and 
candidate campaign committees are allowed to spend 
unlimited amounts (except in the case of presidential 
candidates who accept public financing and thereby 
accept expenditure caps as a qualifying condition for 
the public money). PACs are free to spend whatever 
funds they raise (although any expenditures made on 
behalf of a candidate, unless they are made independ- 
ently of the candidate, count as in-kind contributions 
that are subject to limits). Labor unions and corpora- 
tions can spend freely to communicate political 


184 


information to their members, conduct non-partisan 
voter mobilization efforts, or make independent expen- 
ditures for or against federal candidates. 


The federal regulations governing political party 
finance differ substantially from those applicable to 
other political committees or sources of funding. As in 
the case of other contributions to federal candidates, 
the law sets strict limits on the contributions made and 
received by political party committees in connection 
with federal elections. It further limits the amounts 
parties may spend on behalf of their candidates. But 
the regulations also acknowledge the distinctive nature 
of party organizations by setting forth a number of 
detailed provisions that recognize the close affiliation 
between a party and its candidates, and the parties’ 
traditional role as organizations devoted to encouraging 
voter participation. It is therefore appropriate to 
review the different types of party finance, and to clar- 
ify the differences between party coordinated spending 
and other forms of party spending. 


A. Contribution Limits 


Under the FECA, national party organizations can 
receive annual contributions of no more than $20,000 
from an individual and $15,000 from a PAC. Any 
contribution from an individual, however, is subject to 
the annual limit of $25,000 that is imposed on an individ- 
ual’s aggregate donations to all federal candidates and 
national party committees, including the national party 
organizations (Democratic National Committee and 
Republican National Committee) and the senatorial and 
congressional campaign committees (National Republi- 
can Senatorial Committee, Democratic Senatorial Cam- 
paign Committee, National Republican Congressional 


185 


Committee, and Democratic Congressional Campaign 
Committee). The law also prohibits party committees 
—_ —— corporate or labor union contributions 
© pay for any activities conducted in connection wi 

federal elections. 13 


In addition to restricting contributions to party 
organizations, the law also restricts contributions or 
other forms of direct assistance from party organiza- 
tions. With respect to direct contributions, each na- 
tional, congressional, and state party campaign com- 
mittee can give $5,000 to a House candidate at each 
stage of the election process, including a primary, 
runoff, and general election. So the maximum amount 
that may be contributed, assuming a runoff election in 
addition to a primary and general election, is $15,000. 
The parties’ national and senatorial campaign commit- 
tees can give slightly more, a combined total of $17,500, 
in an election cycle to a Senate candidate. State party 
committees can contribute an additional $5,000 to a 


Senate candidate. None of these limits are adjusted for 
inflation. 


B. Coordinated Expenditures (§441a[d]}) 


| Parties are also allowed to spend money on behalf of 
individual candidates. These outlays, which are 
authorized under 2 U.S.C. §441a(d), are commonly 
known as “coordinated expenditures” because they can 
be made in direct coordination with a federal candidate 
or his or her campaign committee. As with direct party 
contributions to a federal candidate, coordinated expen- 
ditures can be made only from funds received under 
federal contribution limits. These expenditures differ 
from campaign contributions in that both the party and 
the candidate share control of them, giving the party 


186 


_ some influence over how these monies are spent. Unlike 
direct contributions, which are usually monetary dona- 
tions to a candidate, coordinated expenditures: gener- 
ally take the form of some kind of campaign service paid 
for by the party, such as polls, television commercials, 
radio ads, direct mailings, or issue research. Coordi- 
nated spending also differs from direct party contri- 
butions in that such expenditures can be made only in 
connection with a federal candidate’s general election 
campaign. While the parties may spend money on 
behalf of a candidate before the general election period, 
any monies spent earlier in the election cycle count 
against the general election limit—there is no separate 
limit for each stage of the election as is the case with 
party contributions. 


The FECA strictly limits the amount a party com- 
mittee may spend in coordination with a federal candi- 
date. These ceilings are based on formulas established 
for each type of election at the federal level. Under the 
original terms set forth in 1974, national party com- 
mittees could spend up to $10,000 per candidate in a 
House general election campaign, except in those states 
with only one congressional district, in which case the 
limit was $20,000. In Senate general elections, the limit 
was the greater amount of $20,000 or two cents times 
the state’s voting-age population, while the presidential 
election ceiling was simply two cents times the voting 
age population, or approximately $2.9 million in 1974. 
Each of these limits was indexed for inflation, so they 
have increased with each new election cycle. By 1996, 
they were roughly three times their original amounts: 
national party committees could spend $12.3 million on 
behalf of a presidential candidate, or $30,910 for a 
House candidate ($61,820 in a single-district state), and 


187 


from $61,820 in the smallest states, to $1.4 million in 
California on behalf of a Senate candidate. 


State party committees are allowed to spend the 
same coordinated amounts in House and Senate races 
as the national party organizations. They are not al- 
lowed to spend coordinated amounts on the presidential 
race. Moreover, the “state” party limit is actually the 
limit for state and local party organizations in a state; 
the law makes no separate provision for spending by 
county or local party organizations. But since all state 
party committees can be considered affiliated commit- 
tees of the national party organizations, a state party 
may assign their spending authority to a national party 
committee, thus in effect doubling the amount a na- 
tional party committee can spend. These assignments, 
known as “agency agreements,” effectively allow a 
national party committee to spend the total amount 
allowed the national and state and local party com- 
mittees in a particular House or Senate race. 


C. Generic Grass-Roots Party Activity and Non- 
federal Funding (§431(8}[B] and §431[9}{B}) 


The limits on party coordinated spending were first 
put into effect in the 1976 elections, and questions about 
the legal status of different types of party funding im- 
mediately arose. Traditionally, party organizations had 
spent significant sums in each election cycle on activi- 
ties such as grass-roots volunteer organization, voter 
identification efforts, get-out-the-vote programs, ge- 
neri¢c party advertising (messages like “Vote Democ- 
ratic” or “Support Republican Candidates”), and the 
production of bumper stickers, buttons, and slate cards 
that might indirectly benefit federal candidates, but did 
not constitute direct assistance to a particular can- 


188 


didate. Under the 1974 FECA’s original provisions, the 
costs of many of these activities could be considered in- 
kind campaign contributions subject to the law. This 
was especially true of grass-roots campaign materials 
such as bumper stickers, lawn signs, and slate cards 
that mentioned particular federal candidates in a listing 
of party candidates for federal, state, or local office. 
Party leaders became particularly concerned about 
materials that included the party’s presidential ticket, 
since the public financing program established by the 
FECA prevented the parties’ nominees from raising 
additional cempaign contributions for the general 
election period. The FECA therefore had the effect of 
chilling traditional grass-roots and party-building 
activities, and reducing the role of party organizations 
in electoral politics. 


In response to these concerns about the diminished 
role of party organizations, Congress amended the 
FECA in 1979. In an effort to strengthen political 
parties and promote grass-roots political activity, the 
Congress accepted a recommendation made by. the 
Federal Election Commission to ease the restrictions 
placed on party contributions and expenditures. These 
provisions, which are codified in $431 of the Act, 
exempted a narrow set of clearly defined volunteer and 
grass-roots party building activities from the defini- 
tions of “contribution” and “expenditure” set forth in 
the Act, so that parties could now spend ynlimited 
amounts on voter registration and identification, cer- 
tain types of campaign material (such as slate cards), 
and voter turnout programs, without violating the 
limits imposed on party contributions and expenditures 
made in connection with a federal election. 


189 


This exemption for party-building activities was 
further expanded by the FEC in a series of advisory 
opinion rulings, beginning with Advisory Opinion 1978- 
10, that permitted party committees to allocate the 
costs of grass-roots party activities in ways that re- 
flected the proportion of federal and nonfederal 
candidates on the ballot. These rulings allowed national 
party committees to pay for the federal share of their 
administrative costs, voter mobilization program costs, 
and other costs subject to $431 with federally regulated 
funds, while the nonfederal share (the proportion repre- 
senting state and local candidates) could be financed 
with contributions permitted under the relevant state 
law. The FEC thus allowed national party committees 
to raise contributions in amounts and from sources that 
were not permitted under federal contribution limits, 
since most state laws were more permissive than 
federal law with respect to contribution limits. This 
nonfederal funding came to be known as “soft money” 
in modern campaign finance parlance, since it was not 
subject to the “hard” contribution limits established by 
the FECA, 


Generic party-building expenditures, or soft money 
financing (to use the popular terminology), is therefore 
clearly distinguishable from the party coordinated 
spending that occurs under §441a(d). Direct contribu- 
ions to federal candidates or for coordinated expendi- 
tures under §441 must be paid for with federally regu- 
lated funds—no soft money is allowed. Coordinated 
expenditures under $441 can be made on behalf of a 
particular federal candidate; most party expenditures 
under §431 cannot be made on behalf of a particular 
federal candidate (the statute does allow mailings or 


190 


phone banks conducted by volunteers to advocate the 
election of a specific candidate). 


Thus, the primary purpose for which soft money is 
used, other than general overhead and administrative 
expenses and related fundraising costs, is to conduct 
voter identification and turnout drives that are jointly 
sponsored by state and local party organizations. These 
voter mobilization drives: are commonly called “coor- 
dinated campaigns” since they seek to coordinate the 
activities of party organizations and candidates to 
conduct one, centralized party voter turnout program. 
Instead of having the state committee, local committee, 
federal candidates, statewide candidates, and local 
candidates spend funds separately for voter identifica- 
tion and turnout, the party organizations develop one 
centralized voter turnout effort to mobilize party sup- 
porters. This approach reduces the amount of overlap, 
represents a more efficient use of funds, and reduces 
the costs incurred by individual candidate campaigns 
for voter identification and turnout. Such “coordinated 
campaigns,” however, are not to be confused with 
coordinated expenditures made by party organizations 
on behalf of specific federal candidates under $441a(d). 


In 1996, both national parties also spent a significant 
amount of soft money for the first time on issue advo- 
cacy ads and other communications designed to 
promote party issue positions. This was the first 
election cycle in which such communications were a 
focus of soft money spending. These communications, 
which did not expressly advocate the election or defeat 
of particular federal candidates, were used to spread 
messages regarding the parties’ perspectives on the 
Administration’s health care proposal, the 1995 federal 


191 


budget debate, President Clinton’s first term record, 
and issues related to the 1996 congressional races. This 
use of soft money, which has been a source of con- 
troversy, is one way parties communicate their views, 
but it is important to note that it is not related to any 
coordinated spending done by the parties. 


D. Independent Expenditures 


Federal regulations adopted under the FECA have 
always recognized the unique relationship and close 
affiliation between political parties and their candi- 
dates. Accordingly, federal regulations have prohibited 
national and state party committees from making inde- 
pendent expenditures in connection with the general 
election campaigns of federal candidates (11 CFR 
$110.7). But the Supreme Court’s 1996 ruling in Colo- 
rado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. 
FEC (116 S. Ct. 2309) concluded that political parties 
can make independent expenditures on behalf of candi- 
dates in congressional races in accordance with their 
First Amendment right of free speech. Consequently, 
party senatorial campaign committees and some state 
party committees established legally separate political 
operations during the general election campaign period 
to make independent expenditures, mostly on behalf of 
Senate candidates. Overall, these committees combined 
to spend $11.5 million on independent expenditures 
during the short period available to them before the 
1996 elections were held. 


As this brief review demonstrates, the rules gov- 
erning political party financial activity are complex. 
Because parties are involved in almost every aspect of 
the elections process and share a unique association 
with federal candidates, the rules must make provision 


192 


for different types of activity to accommodate the 
parties’ diverse functions. It is therefore important, 
when speaking about party finance, to distinguish the 
type of funding involved and the particular issues 
associated with that form of funding. 


Ill. The Role of Party Coordinated Expenditures 


In Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Com- 
mittee v. FEC, the Court expressed specific interest in 
party coordinated expenditures and in the question of 
whether it is constitutionally permissible to limit such 
spending. This question is best answered by first 
examining how parties spend funds in coordination with 
federal candidates, and summarizing the effects of these 
expenditures in federal contests. 


As noted in the review of party financing above, 
coordinated expenditures are the only form of party 
spending limited under the FECA. These ceilings were 
established in 1974, when the FECA established a 
regulatory regime that limited all forms of political 
spending in an effort to curb rising campaign costs and 
equalize the resources available to candidates. These 
limits on party spending ensured that the ceilings 
placed on candidate spending would be effective. 


In 1976, the Supreme Court’s decision in Buckley v. 
Valeo struck down most of the spending limits that the 
party coordinated spending limits were designed to 
support. The Court ruled that limits on congressional 
campaign spending were unconstitutional, since they 
limited political speech without serving the compelling 
state interest of preventing corruption or the appear- 
ance of corruption. On the same grounds, the Court 
overruled the limits that had been set on independent 


193 


expenditures and on the right of a candidate to spend 
personal funds on his or her own campaign for federal 
office. However, the limits the FECA imposed on 
party coordinated expenditures were not challenged in 
this case. They therefore remained in effect after the 
Buckley decision, even though there was no evidence to 
suggest that these limits were needed to protect the 
integrity of the electoral process. The result was an 
imbalance in the campaign finance system—while 
wealthy candidates using their own resources could 
spend unlimited amounts on their campaigns, and well- 
financed incumbents could spend unlimited amounts 
and PACs and other groups or individuals could spend 
unlimited amounts independently in federal elections, 
party committees were restricted in the amounts they 
could spend to assist their candidates. 


Not only did Congress limit the amounts parties 
could. spend in coordination with their candidates, but 
they also set the ceilings at unrealistically low levels. 
The ceilings were not designed to ensure effective or 
meaningful political communication. They were set at 
levels—2 cents times the voting-age population with 
adjustments for inflation—that virtually guaranteed 
that any communication with party members on behalf 
of candidates would be severely limited. At this level of 
funding, party committees could not afford to pay the 
postage on one letter to each eligible voter in a district 
or state, never mind the cost of the stationary and 
printing needed to produce the letter. In fact, the limits 
were so low that a party committee could not even send 
a letter to each party supporter in a state or district; 
that is, it could not pay to send one letter to its own 
members. In contrast, congressional incumbents in 
1974 were spending $30.5 million posting franked mail 


194 


to constituents at an average cost of about 10 cents per 
piece of mail. By 1988, congressional incumbents were 
spending about $82.2 million on franked mail, at an 
average mailing expense of about 14 cents. The coor- 
dinated spending allowed party organizations, however, 
had only increased to about 5 cents for each person of 
voting age. 


The coordinated spending limits are particularly 
restrictive because they are adjusted only for inflation 
and voting-age population. Neither of these measures 
adequately represents the real changes in the costs of 
campaigns. The costs of campaign services and com- 
munications, such as polling, television advertising, 
direct mail services, and travel expenses, have in- 
creased at a rate significantly greater than the Con- 
sumer Price Index, which is used to adjust party 
spending limits. For example, between 1976 and 1980, 
the Consumer Price Index rose about 40 percent, but 
the cost of such basic items as mass mailings, television 
commercials, and air travel grew by 50, 100, and 300 
percent. Between 1976 and 1982, the McCann-Erickson 
index to media advertising costs, which includes all 
advertising mediums, rose at a rate that was 14 percent 
greater than inflation. Such increases led FEC Com- 
missioner Lee Ann Elliott to observe in 1992 that “in 
truth, campaign costs don’t go up at marketbasket 
inflation; instead of going up about four or five percent 
every year, they go up ten, twelve, fifteen percent every 
year” (Elliott, 1992:302, emphasis in original). 


As for voting-age population, this adjustment is only 
applied to a party’s spending limit in senate elections 
and those states with a single congressional district. As 
noted above (see section II.b), congressional races in 


195 


multi-district states are subject to a limit originally set 
at $10,000, which is only adjusted for inflation, even 
though the mean population of a congressional district 
has grown from approximately 470,000 when the FECA 
was adopted to more than 572,000 in 1990. This dispar- 
ity in the treatment of congressional district spending 
limits has created significant variations in the amounts 
a party may spend in different congressional districts. 
For example, between 1976 and 1996 the amount a 
party committee could spend in most congressional dis- 
tricts (those in multi-district states) rose to an inflation- 
adjusted $30,910, with no accommodation for the 
average growth in a district’s size. In a single-district 
state like Wyoming, however, a party could spend up to 
$61,820, the same amount that could be spent on behalf 
of a senate candidate, or more than twice the amount 
permitted in other congressional districts. Thus, in 
1996, a party could spend no more than $30,910 to help a 
congressional candidate in New York, where congres- 
sional districts have an average voting-age population 
of 438,700, but could spend $61,820 to help a candidate 
in Wyoming, where the voting-age population totals 
approximately 344,000. The formula for determining 
party spending limits also creates inequities between 
single-district states. For example, in 1996, a party 
men spend a same amount in Wyoming and Mon- 
ana, even though Montana’s voting-age 

(634,000) is almost twice as large. —— 


Despite the constraints imposed by the inadequacies 
of the limits, party organizations recognize the value of 
coordinated spending and devote significant sums of 
money to this activity. On average, since 1986, the 
Democrats have dedicated about 13 percent of their 
total federal spending to coordinated spending. The 


196 


Republicans, who in recent elections have raised about 
twice as much federal money as the Democrats, have 
disbursed about 9 percent of their total federal spend- 
ing in a coordinated manner. Since 1990, the Democ- 
ratie and Republican parties have each spent at least 
$20 million to $20 million per election on coordinated 
expenditures. This represents a substantial increase 
from the levels of the early 1980s. For example, the 
Democrats spent $4.9 million on coordinated expendi- 
tures in 1980 and $3.3 million in 1982; the Republicans 
spent $12.4 million and $14.3 million, respectively. By 
contrast, the major parties spend only 1-2 percent of 
their federal funds on direct contributions to candi- 
dates, or an average in recent elections of $2 million to 
$3.5 million per election. (For a summary of national 
party federal financial activity, see Table 1.) 


Generally, the four national congressional commit- 
tees or “Hill committees” (the Democratic Senatorial 
Campaign Committee [DSCC], Democratic Congres- 
sional Campaign Committee [DCCC], National Republi- 
can Senatorial Committee [NRSC], and National 
Republican Congressional Committee [NRCC]) take 
the lead in working with state party committees to 
effectuate coordinated expenditures made in connection 
with congressional elections. The national and state 
party committees work together to determine how best 
to allocate their resources to provide the greatest 
benefit to their candidates. In some instances, the 
national committees work with state organizations to 
combine their spending authority; in other cases, they 
offer guidance to state parties engaged in their own 
activities. 


197 


As noted in Table 3, coordinated expenditures are a 
major activity of national and state and local party 
committees in both the Democratic and Republican 
parties. The total amounts spent on such activity have 
increased substantially in recent election cycles, with 
the largest share financed by the national party 
committees. While they lack the resources available to 
their national counterparts, state and local parties also 
play an important role in effectuating coordinated 
activity. These committees have spent significant sums 
on coordinated expenditures, especially when con- 
sidered in relation to their total election-related 
spending. They have also demonstrated a growing 
capacity to assist their candidates in this way, as 
indicated by the increasingly large sums devoted to 
such spending in the last three election cycles. In the 
1992 and 1994 election cycles, Republican state com- 
mittees spent an average of $1 million on behalf of 
federal candidates, while Democratic committees spent 
an average of over $1.4 million. 


The hard monies that are used for coordinated expen- 
ditures are generated through ongoing fundraising op- 
erations that include direct mail solicitations, tele- 
marketing efforts, party fundraisers, an annual con- 
gressional dinner, and the solicitation of individual 
donors. The vast majority of the monies used for 
coordinated expenditures are generated through contri- 
butions of relatively small amounts from individual 
donors. In fact, most of the hard money raised by party 
committees for use in connection with federal elections 
is received in contributions that fall far below the 
maximum allowable amounts. Because party con- 
tributions are subject to the annual aggregate $25,000 
ceiling on individual giving, very few donors give 


198 


amounts that are even close to the legal maximum. Of 
the more than one million individuals who donate 
money to national party committees each election cycle, 
there are probably fewer than a thousand donors, both 
parties included, who give anywhere near the maxi- 
mum individual contribution of $20,000 in any given 
election cycle. State and local party committees are 
subject to even smaller hard money contribution limits. 
These committees may accept no more than $5,000 total 
from an individual for activities conducted on behalf of 


federal candidates. 


Decisions about coordinated spending are made by 
party leaders and staff members, working in conjunc- 
tion with candidates. The chairs, vice chairs, and 
members of the Hill committees are members of Con- 
gress who are selected by their colleagues in the House 
and Senate. In addition, the committees have full-time 
staffs: in 1996, the DSCC had 38 staff members, the 
NRCC and DCCC 64 each, and the NRSC 150. The 
elected members of the committee essentially act as a 
board of directors, establishing priorities and assisting 
in the recruitment of candidates, fundraising, and other 
activities. The staff members oversee the daily 
operations and play a major role in formulating party 
strategies and implementing those strategies. 


The administrative apparatus needed to effectuate 
coordinated expenditures is therefore very expensive 
to operate. During the 1996 election cycle, roughly 50 
percent of the DCCC’s funds and 45 percent of the 
DSCC’s monies were used to finance staff salaries, com- 
puters, media studios, voter lists, fundraising programs, 
loan repayments, and other overhead costs. The NRCC 
and NRSC spent even more on these activities, 


199 


committing approximately 55 percent and 48 percent of 
their funds to them. n ; 


A. Enhancing Electoral Competition 


The national party Hill committees have a common 
overriding goal: they seek to maximize the number of 
seats their party holds in Congress. Party leaders and 
committee staffs therefore try to make the most 
efficient and effective use of their available resources to 
win elections. While the membership of these com- 
mittees would suggest that they would favor incum- 
bents in distributing their resources, since the members 
are sitting members of the House and Senate, the 
reality is just the opposite. Instead of focusing on 
incumbents, party committees focus on candidates in 
close contests or marginal districts where party 
assistance might make a difference in whether the 
party candidate wins or loses the election. They also 
concentrate on challengers who can wage competitive 
campaigns against an entrenched incumbents of the 


opposite party. However, federal law hamstrings these 
efforts. 


. Party resources are not a major source of funding for 
incumbents. The bulk of party contributions and 
expenditures are devoted to candidates for open seats 
(races where an incumbent is not seeking reelection or 
loses a primary election and its therefore not a 
candidate in the general election), and challengers who 
are running in competitive contests or who might 
become competitive with party assistance. Sometimes 
the parties do provide some assistance to incumbents 
because they have the resources available to do so. For 
example, in 1984 the NRCC had sufficient funds to give 
all of its incumbents the maximum legal direct con- 


200) 


tribution, regardless of the competitiveness of their 
contests. In recent elections, the NRSC has usually 
given the maximum legal contribution to all of its 
candidates, and the DSCC has given the maximum to 
most of its candidates because they had the resources 
to do so. But in most of these instances, the party 
organizations did not provide incumbents with further 
assistance by making significant coordinated expendi- 
tures on their behalf. 


National political and economic conditions can also 
dictate a slightly greater distribution of resources to 
incumbents. According to Paul Herrnson, an authority 
on party spending in congressional elections, when 


. 
national conditions do not favor the president’s party, - 


the in-party committees take a more “defensive” pos- 
ture towards incumbents, and the out-party committees 
use more of their resources to help non-incumbents. 
When national conditions favor the president’s party, 
the patterns are reversed, with the in-party committees 
giving more to non-incumbents and the out-party 
spending more to support incumbents. But even when 
parties support incumbents, it usually helps mainly 
those who are in jeopardy of losing their seats 
(Herrnson, 1995:81). 


That party committees focus their spending on non- 
incumbents who most need their support can be 
determined by examining the disclosure reports on 
coordinated spending issued by the Federal Election 
Commission. In order to determine the amounts of 
money given by the parties to different types of candi- 
dates (incumbents, challengers, or those seeking: open 
seats), | aggregated the amounts spent by committees 
of each major party in coordination with the general 


201 


election candidates in House and Senate contests dur- 
ing the 1996 general election, as disclosed in the 
itemized candidate-by-candidate listing released by the 
Federal Election Commission in its March 1997 press 
release on party spending. (See Federal Election 
Commission 1997a, pages 12-34.) I then determined the 
total and average amounts spent by each party on 
behalf of incumbents, challengers, and open seat can- 
didates in each type of federal election (House, Senate, 
or President). The results of this analysis follow. 


While there are times when an incumbent in a safe 
seat may receive party resources, these candidates 
account for relatively little of the coordinated spending 
done by party committees on behalf of their candidates. 
The majority of coordinated expenditures are made to 
help non-incumbents. Indeed, the general spending 
patterns that characterized the 1996 election cycle are 


fairly typical of the patterns evident in recent years 
(see Table 4). 


Overall, Democratic party committees disbursed 
more than $22 million on coordinated expenditures in 
the 1996 election cycle, as compared to more than $30 
million for the Republicans. The Democrats spent $6.7 
million in coordination with their presidential candi- 
date, $8.6 million in coordination with 28 Senate candi- 
dates, and $7 million in coordination with 316 House 
candidates. The Republicans spent $11.7 million in 
coordination with their presidential candidate, around 
$11 million in 32 Senate races, and about $8 million in 
156 House races. 


In both parties in 1996, the majority of party coor- 
dinated expenditures were made on behalf of non- 
incumbents. The Democrats devoted 85 percent of 


202 


their total coordinated expenditures in Senate races to 
challengers (31 percent) and open seat candidates (54 
percent). In House races, 80 percent was spent on 
behalf of non-incumbents, with challengers (56 percent) 
receiving more than twice the share that went to open 
seat candidates (24 percent). Almost all of the money 
spent on behalf of incumbents—15 percent in Senate 
races and 20 percent in House races—went to incum- 
bents who were in jeopardy of losing their seats. 


The average dollar amounts spent by Democratic 
party committees on behalf of candidates also signifi- 
cantly favored non-incumbents. In House races, party 
coordinated spending averaged $39, 852 on behalf of 
open seat candidates, $26,065 on behalf of challengers, 
and $11,595 on behalf of incumbents. In Senate races, 
open seats received an average of $357,117 in spending, 
challengers $263,880 in spending, and incumbents 
$266,116. Spending on behalf of senate incumbents was 
slightly higher than the average spent on challengers 
due to the small number of incumbents seeking reelec- 
tion, all of whom were involved in highly competitive 
contests. 


The Republicans’ coordinated activity also favored 
non-incumbents. The Republican committees, however, 
spent more than the Democrats defending incumbents 
because of the unusually lar, number of incumbents 
involved in close races, especially among the large 
freshman class in the House, most of whom won in 1994 
with 55 percent of the vote or less. Even with this 
unusually large number of threatened incumbents, the 
Senate and House committees each disbursed close to 
two-thirds of their coordinated funds on races involving 
non-incumbents. In Senate races, 63 percent of 


203 


coordinated expenditures were made on behalf of non- 
incumbents, with challengers receiving 21 percent of 
the total amount expended and open seat candidates, 42 
percent. In the House elections, where 63 percent of 
the funds also went to non-incumbents,. challengers 
received 34 percent of the coordinated money, while 
open seat candidates received 29 percent. 


The average dollar amounts spent on behalf of 
candidates by Republican party committees were more 
balanced than the comparable sums for Democrats, but 
still favored non-incumbents, albeit only by small differ- 
ences in some instances. In House races, Republican 
committee disbursed an average of $56,030 on behalf of 
candidates in open seat races, $52,683 for challengers, 
and $47,017 for incumbents. In Senate races, open seat 
spending once again led the way, with these candidates 
receiving an average of $359,874, as compared to 
$335,758 for challengers and $335,289 for incumbents. 


The 1996 allocations are similar to those in previous 
cycles. In 1992, for example, national party committees 
allocated a majority of their funds to non-incumbents. 
The Democratic committees allocated 76 percent of the 
monies spent on Senate races to challengers (47 per- 
cent) and open seat candidates (29 percent), while 56 
percent of the funds disbursed in connection with 
House races went to challengers (36 percent) and open 
seat candidates (20 percent). On the Republican side, 53 
percent of the monies allocated for Senate races went to 
challengers (24 percent) and open seat candidates (29 
percent), while 75 percent of the monies disbursed in 
connection with House races went to challengers (44 
percent) and open seat candidates (31 percent) (See 


204 


Herrnson, 1995:86, figures include contributions and 
coordinated expenditures). 


That most of the monies spent in coordination with 
candidates are targeted on non-incumbents and close 
races makes sense, given the purpose these party funds 
serve in the electoral process. Party committees rely 
on their coordinated expenditures to help the maximum 
number of candidates.win election. That is, they dis- 
burse these funds in an effort to help their candidates, 
especially challengers, gain access to the resources they 
need to wage succesful campaigns. Party funding thus 
helps to make elections more competitive. It enhances 


the ability of non-incumbents to contest elections, | 


increase their name recognition, and make their views 
known to the electorate. In this way, parties help to 
improve the choices available to electorate and enhance 
the vitality of the electoral process. 


Party coordinated expenditures are especially im- 
portant because parties are the only source of funding 
that mostly benefits non-incumbents. As political 
scientists have noted for years, one of the major prob- 
lems in the campaign finance system is the inability of 
challengers to raise the monies needed to wage viable 
campaigns. For challengers who are not wealthy and 
capable of financing a campaign from their personal 
resources, there are only three sources of campaign 
funding available to them under the FECA: contribu- 
tions from individuals, donations from PACs, or money 
from political parties. Most of the individual gifts go to 
incumbents, who have well-established donor bases and 
the advantage of incumbency. And the vast majority of 
PAC contributions also go to incumbents. In fact, in 
recent elections, PACs have given an average of more 


205 


than 70 percent of their campaign contributions te 
incumbents, as opposed to only 10-15 percent to chal- 
lengers (the balance, about 18 percent, goes to open 
seat races). In 1996, for example, PACs gave $146.4 
million to incumbents, compared to $31.6 million to their 
challengers. In 1994, the difference was $137.2 million 
to $19 million; in 1992, $135.3 million to $22.9 million; 
and in 1990, $125.8 million to $16.2 million. 


Challengers therefore look to party committees for 
assistance in overcoming the significant financial dis- 
advantages they experience in most elections. Party 
money is the only major source of funds in the political 
system that challengers can rely on to help counter the 
overwhelming resource advantage incumbents enjoy as 
a result of their greater success in fundraising, espe- 
cially in soliciting PAC contributions. It also helps to 
counter the advantage incumbents enjoy as a result of 


the privileges that accompany their position in gov- 


ernment, such as the franking privilege . 


The problem facing parties, however, is that under 
the current campaign finance laws, they are strictly 
limited in the amount they can spend in coordination 
with their candidates. As a result, although they can 
provide some assistance to party candidates challenging 
incumbents, they cannot hope to match the advantages 
incumbents receive from PACs. For example, in 1996, 
PACs gave an average of over $950,000 to Senate 
incumbents, more than three times the average amount 
they gave Senate challengers, and far more than the 
average amount spent by party committees on chal- 
lengers’ behalf. PACs gave an average of close to 
$300,000 to House incumbents, which was more than 
five times the amount given challengers and approxi- 


206 


mately five times more than the amount that national 
and state parties combined were legally allowed to 
spend ($61,820) on behalf of their challengers. 


The current limits on party expenditures thus reduce 
the level of electoral competition by constraining 
parties in their ability to make their candidates more 
competitive. In many instances, parties would be 
willing to spend more to assist their candidates, but are 
prevented from doing so because of federal ceilings. In 
1996, for example, the Democratic and Republican con- 
gressional campaign committees essentially spent the 
maximum allowable coordinated amount—$61,820 total 
for party committees at all levels of government—in 
approximately 150 House races. That is, the com- 
mittees spent 95 percent or more of the limit in races 
involving 103 non-incumbents, including 60 candidates 
challenging incumbents, and 44 incumbents. All of these 
incumbents were involved in close races; all except for 
two (Republicans Hilleary of Tennessee and 
Christensen of Nebraska, who finished with 58 and 56 
percent of the general election vote, respectively) 
received 55 percent of the vote or less in the general 
election. 


The limits had an even greater effect in restraining 
spending in Senate races. In 1996, there were 68 major 
party candidates seeking election for 34 seats. Of these 
68 candidates, party committees disbursed the maxi- 
mum amount in coordinated expenditures, in 41 cases, 
or 60 percent. Republican committees reached the limit 
in 25 races involving 12 open seat candidates, 5 
challengers, and 8 incumbents. Democrats reached the 
limit in 16 races invoiving 10 open seat candidates, 5 
challengers, and 1 incumbent. That the party com- 


207 


mittees are more apt to reach the limit in Senate races 
is understandable, since in most instances the quality of 
the candidates who seek election to the Senate tends to 
make these highly competitive races. Moreover, in 
these races candidates need to communicate their views 
with statewide electorates, rather than the voters in a 
single congressional district, and given the costs of 
communicating with such a large audience, taken 
together with the inadequacy of the coordinated 
spending limits with respect to the real costs of 
campaigning, it is not surprising that the ceiling is 
quickly achieved in most cases. 


B. Facilitating Political Speech 


How do parties disburse the monies they are allowed 
to spend in coordination with candidates under 
$44la(d)? In what specific ways do they seek to help 
their candidates? 


Party committees offer candidates a wide range of 
election-related services, including organizational con- 
sulting, issue research, polling, mail services, and media 
production and advertising assistance. Generally, party 
committee leaders and staff members work with 
candidates and their campaign staffs to provide them 
with the resources and type of assistance they need 
most. That is, the party works with each candidate to 
determine how coordinated funds can best be spent to 
improve that candidate’s prospect of winning election. 
Parties therefore pay for different campaign services 
on behalf of different candidates, depending on a 
candidate’s particular situation and needs. But what 
candidates need most, especially non-incumbents, is 
help in broadcasting their message and communicating 


208 


their views so that they are known by the voters who 
will cast ballots on Election Day. 


Party coordinated funding is used almost exclusively 
to help candidates communicate more effectively with 
the public. Parties and candidates both know that 
voter communication is the key to winning elections. 
Candidates must develop a message that is meaningful 
to voters and have the resources needed to present that 
message to the electorate. To have their voices heard 
in modern election campaigns, candidates rely on tele- 
vision or radio advertising, or direct mail, the latter 
being the preferred medium for many congressional 
candidates, since broadcast vehicles like television or 
radio usually have audiences that span more than one 
congressional district and are therefore less efficient 
and more costly means of communicating than targeted 
mail to likely voters. But all of these methods of 
communicating are expensive, particularly given the 
fact that Senate and congressional candidates must 
disseminate their views on a number of different issues 
during the course of a campaign. 


Non-incumbents, particularly challengers running 
against well-known incumbents, often find it difficult to 
raise the funds needed to make their views known to 
the electorate and gain the name recognition among the 
voters that is required for a viable or winning cam- 
paign. Party organizations therefore try as much as 
possible to promote their candidates’ views and en- 
hance their ability to speak with voters. In doing so, a 
party fulfills one of its principle objectives, since the 
main way a party’s ideology and policy perspectives 
become known to the electorate, and thus the primary 
way it generates public support, is through the speech 


209 


of its candidates. Parties therefore have a keen interest 


in promoting the widespread airing of their candidates’ 
views. 


The national party committees assist candidates with 
campaign communications in a number of ways. The 
predominant approach is to provide candidates with the 
funding needed to broadcast their messages or post 
letters to selected voters within their districts. But this 
is not the only means by which party committees 
support their candidates’ speech. Both the DCCC and 
the NRCC have state-of-the-art television and radio 
production facilities on their premises, which are used 
to give candidates technical and editorial assistance in 
producing television and radio ads. The committees 
also have satellite capabilities that allow candidates to 
beam television communications back to their districts 
and interact “live” with voters. 


One way the parties use these facilities is to produce 
“generic” ads each election cycle that include general 
party messages and themes, which can be customized 
for particular candidates by incorporating a candidate’s 
name, picture, and voice. These generic ads are 
normally used by challengers who lack the resources to 
hire a media consulting firm or hope to reduce their 
media production costs by using one of these party ads. 


Candidates can also use the party media production 
facilities to make ads for themselves. Both congres- 
sional committees have staff members available to offer 
technical and editorial advice, particularly guidance in 
identifying major themes, drafting scripts, and produc- 
ing ads. The senate committees, the DSCC and NRSC, 
take a less active role in developing candidate commu- 
nications, since senate candidates usually have the 


210 


experience and funding needed to develop public rela- 
tions strategies, in addition to media consultants who 
handle most of their ad production. However, these 


committees still play a role in their candidates’ cam- - 


paign communications. The NSRC provides its clien- 
tele with radio actuality services that enable candidates 
to record a campaign message that local stations can 
access by dialing a toll-free number. The NRSC and 
DSCC also sponsor Internet sites that distribute 
general information about their Senate contenders. 


The extent to which party committees assist candi- 
dates with their campaign speech and the emphasis 
placed on promoting candidate views when allocating 
coordinated expenditures under §44la(d) can be 
determined by examining the disclosure reports filed 
by the party committees and reported by the Federal 
Election Commission. Under federal disclosure re- 
quirements, each party committee must provide an 
itemized listing of its coordinated expenditures, noting 
the candidate on whose behalf an expenditure is made, 
the payee, the amount, and the purpose of the expendi- 
ture. These itemized disbursements are reported in the 
monthly reports filed by the party committees on 
Schedule F and line 25 of the committees’ summary 
reports. By reviewing each of these expenditures, the 
amounts spent on media, mail, research, polling, and 
other particulars can be easily determined. 


Parties use their coordinated expenditures under 
$441a(d) to help their candidates communicate with 
voters. Over 90 percent of the monies spent by national 
party committees on behalf of their candidates is spent 
on political communication. And most of the other 10 
percent is spent on issues research and polling, which is 


211 


used to help candidates develop and target their cam- 
paign messages. So this spending is also directly related 
to political communication. 


For example, in 1992 the NRCC and the Republican 
National Committee (RNC) spent a combined total of 
almost $6 million in coordination with Republican 
House candidates. Of this total, at least $5.5 million, or 
over 91 percent, was spent to help candidates share 
their views with the voters, either by supporting 
broadcast via electronic media or by paying the costs of 
direct mail. The NRCC distributed roughly $1.7 million 
in electronic media services (television and radio) 
to Republican House candidates, while the Republican 
National Committee (RNC) spent an additional 
$613,000. The NRCC produced 188 television advertise- 
ments for 45 candidates, 23 of whom were incumbents
