and 22 challenger or open seat candidates. Two-thirds 
of these ads were created specifically for individual 
candidates, with the remainder consisting of generic 
party ads that were customized for individual can- 
didates. The committee also produced radio ads for 45 
House members and 44 nonincumbents. In addition, 
the party spent $3.2 million on direct mail advertising in 
coordination with candidates. 


The DCCC distributed approximately $4.1 million in 
$441a(d) funds to its 1992 candidates, approximately 94 
percent of which was used for candidate communi- 
cations. The committee disbursed roughly $3.2 million 
worth of television and radio services to a large number 
of candidates. Over 170 House members and 70 non- 
incumbents used the Democrats’ Harriman Commu- 
nications Center to record and edit their television 
commercials. Others used the committee’s facilities to 


212 


record radio advertisements. In addition, the Demo- 
crats provided their candidates with $725,000 in direct 
mail services 


The Senate committees spent significantly more than 
House committees on behalf of their 1992 candidates, 
but the shares devoted to direct candidate communi- 
cations were roughly the same, with at least 9 out of 
every 10 dollars spent in coordination with a candidate 
being used to distribute candidate messages to the 
voters. The DSCC disbursed $9.6 million of its $11.2 
million in total coordinated spending on electronic 
media services for Democratic candidates. It spent an 
additional $390,000 on direct mail services for Senate 
candidates. The NRSC dedicated almost 97 percent of 
its $44la(d) spending to direct candidate communi- 
cations. It disbursed nearly $15 million of its $16.5 
million in coordinated expenditures to television and 
radio advertising for Senate candidates, and another 
$1.1 million on direct mail. 


Similar patterns characterized the parties’ coordi- 
nated expenditure distributions in 1996. The NRCC, 
for example, spent a total of $7.3 million. Of this 
amount, approximately 99 percent was spent on media, 
mail, and telephone services used to help candidates 
communicate with voters. The committee disbursed 
approximately $4.2 million on media services. It spent 
an additional $3 million on direct mail, and over 
$150,000 on telephone communications. The DSCC 
spent about $8.4 million on behalf of Democratic Senate 
candidates, 90 percent of which was used for media 
advertising and mail. The Democrats spent $7 million 
on media advertising, and almost $500,000 on mail. Most 
of the remaining funds were used for polling. 


213 


The major exception to the general pattern of recent 
elections was the allocation of resources by the DCCC 
in 1996. This committee spent $7 million on behalf of 
congressional candidates, with 70 percent of this 
amount dedicated to direct candidate communications 
and the remaining share spent almost exclusively on 
polling and issues research. The DCCC spent almost 
$4.4 million on media services. The committee gave 
virtually every Democratic House candidate $20,000 in 
credits that could be redeemed at its Harriman Com- 
munications Center, and its staff estimated that 
roughly 70 percent of their candidates redeemed them. 
The party committee also produced 12 different generic 
ads that were essentially used by non-incumbents who 
could not afford to hire a media consultant. The DCCC 
also spent $512,000 on mail and telephone services. 


The reason why the Democrats disbursed a smaller, 
although still very significant, share of their coordi- 
nated expenditures on direct candidate communications 
is because they spent a substantial amount on issues 
research and polling to help their candidates develop 
their messages. As the minority party in Congress, the 
Democrats fielded a larger number of challengers, 
many of whom faced first-term incumbents who had 
received less than 55 percent of the vote in 1994. The 
Democrats therefore spent over 20 percent of their 
coordinated monies working with candidates to provide 
them with issues research, voting record information, 
and polling data to help them define the major issues in 
the race and identify messages that could be used to 
frame their candidacies. Much of this money was - 
disbursed in the summer and early September, when 
many challengers were organizing their general 
election campaigns. 


214 


Candidates, especially non-incumbents, place great 
value on coordinated party funding, since it gives them 
opportunities to spread their message that they would 
not be able to afford otherwise. But the limits on party 
spending make it impossible for these committees to 
pay for a very extensive or intensive amount of 
campaign speech. While the parties spend substantial 
sums on coordinated expenditures under $441la(d), 
almost all of which goes toward political communi- 
cations, the assistance they provide to individual 
candidates is relatively minor. As noted earlier, even if 
a party takes advantage of bulk mail and reduced postal 
rates, and spends the maximum amount allowed the 
national and state and local committees in a given 
congressional district, the party can at best pay the 
pestage for one mailing to a targeted group of voters 
within a district. It is not allowed to pay the production 
expenses and mailing costs of just one letter to the 
voters in a district. 


Nor can the party finance a significant amount of 
media broadcast time. Generally, the 441la(d) limits 
prevent the parties from purchasing the broadcast time 
needed to air more than one or two ads effectively on a 
statewide basis. For example, in 1996, the national 
party organizations were allowed to spend $168,707 in 
South Carolina under the $44la(d) ceiling. The 
estimated cost to air one thirty-second campaign ad in 
South Carolina on a statewide basis with enough 
frequency that it would penetrate the viewing audience 
and have its message understood by voters was 
approximately $186,000. Similarly, in Georgia, the 
estimated cost of a statewide media buy for one ad was 
$280,000 and the state spending limit was $526,224; in 
Colorado, $123,750 against a limit of $170,932; and in 


215 


Massachusetts, over $250,000 against a limit of 
$286,968. Party committees are therefore usually 
forced to be selective in financing candidate advertis- 
ing, paying for the purchase of time only in selected 
markets and for short periods of time. Often they must 
decide whether to finance early media broadcasts in 
hopes of giving a candidate, especially nonincumbents, a 
boost by providing some early voter attention and 
momentum. Or they must choose an alternative 
strategy and finance some late media broadcasts in the 
final week or two of a campaign in hopes of giving 
candidates greater visibility in the crucial final stages of 
an election. Given the constraints of the law, parties 
usually cannot do both. 


The limits on party coordinated spending thus force 
candidates and parties alike to rely on other sources of 
revenue to finance their campaigns. These limits make 
PAC money more attractive to candidates, since 
candidates can receive as much PAC money as they can 
raise, although they can take no more than $10,000 
($5,000 in the primary election and $5,000 in the general 
election) from any one PAC. These limits also place 
many candidates at a serious competitive disadvantage, 
especially challengers facing a well-financed incumbent 
or a candidate facing a wealthy opponent who is 
financing his or her campaign from unlimited personal 
funds, since they cannot look to the party for the 
supplemental assistance they may need to communicate 
effectively with voters in a campaign environment in 
which they are being outspent significantly by their 
well-financed opponent. 


The limit on party spending also forces party com- 
mittees to seek alternative means of assisting can- 


216 


didates in races where the coordinated spending limit 
has already been reached. One means available is to 
increase the amount spent in a district or state under 
$431 of the Act. This would allow parties to spend 
monies on voter turnout and general party campaign 
materials such as slate cards and sample ballots, but 
this is an inferior means of communicating with voters 
as compared to direct advocacy of a candidate through 
broadcast advertising or broad-based mailings. More- 
over, any spending under $431 can be financed in part 
with soft money funds that are not subject to federal 
contribution limits. The choice of this alternative would 
thus encourage parties to raise more soft money, which 
is subject to less effective disclosure and no con- 
tribution limits. 


The other alternative available to parties would be to 
shift the allocation of their hard money resources from 
coordinated expenditures made in concert with their 
candidates to independent expenditures made without 
any coordination with their candidates. But this too is a 
less desirable alternative. As discussed in the next 
section, this alternative would allow parties to freely 
advocate the election or defeat of particular candidates, 
but only at the cost of increased party fundraising 
demands, a weakening of the link between parties and 
their candidates, and the possibility of voter confusion 
and a reduced level of accountability in the political 
system. 


C. Coordinated Expenditures versus Independent 
Expenditures 


The Supreme Court’s 1996 ruling in Colorado Repu- 
blican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Elec- 
tion Commission has offered political parties an alter- 


217 


native means of communicating with the electorate 
about their candidates: they can spend money indepen- 
dently. Under the Court’s ruling, parties can make 
independent expenditures without having these dis- 
bursemen.s count against federal contribution limits or 
coordinated spending ceilings. Parties can therefore 
avoid the restrictions imposed on coordinated spending 
by spending monies independently in support of their 
candidates. By undertaking independent expenditures, 
parties can expressly advocate the election of their 
candidates for federal office and freely communicate 
with the electorate without restrictions on their right to 
free speech. But this approach constitutes a much less 
effective means of communicating with voters, and it 
would increase the fundraising demands on party 
organizations, while at the same time reducing the level 
of accountability and responsiveness in the electoral 
process. 


In 1996, the parties responded to the Supreme 
Court’s ruling by making independent expenditures in 
federal election contests for the first time. Prior to the 
Court’s ruling, federal campaign finance regulations 
had prevented parties from spending funds independ- 
ently of their candidates to expressly advocate their 
election or the defeat of their opponents. Questions 
concerning the legal procedures and arrangements 
needed to make independent expenditures in the 
absence of promulgated regulations led the parties to 
proceed with caution in 1996. In fact, the Democrats 
spent most of August and September seeking clarifica- 
tion of the law, and therefore did not establish an inde- 
pendent expenditures operation until the final weeks of 
the campaign. However, despite the uncertainty and 
short time period available between the issuing of the 


218 


Court’s opinion and Election Day, the parties still 
managed to spend $11.5 million independent!y, with the 
Republicans spending $10 million as conipared to $1.5 
million for the Democrats. 


In 1996, almost all of the monies spent independently 
by party committees were spent in Senate races, 
primarily open seat races in which the party had 
already spent the maximum legal amount in coordi- 
nated spending. Of the $11.5 million total for both 
parties, over $8.3 million was spent in Senate open seat 
contests and only about $118,000 was spent in House 
races. Given the unrealistically low ceilings on coor- 
dinated spending and the large number of races in 
which parties reach the limit, it is likely that parties 
will allocate an increasing amount of money to inde- 
pendent expenditure efforts in the future. And in 
pursuing this option, it is inevitable that candidates and 
parties alike will face legal a range of legal questions 
that will have to be resolved. 


The problem with independent expenditures is that 
they are less efficient and effective than coordinated 
expenditures as a means of communicating with voters. 
First, in order to ensure that any independent expendi- 
tures made by a party are independent of other party 
activities, party organizations in 1996 each established a 
legally separate entity that was solely responsible for 
making independent expenditures. Each party had to 
rent and furnish an office, hire staff, and pay other 
administrative costs. They had to engage additional 
consulting services, and duplicate many of the functions 
already being undertaken by other party offices. The 
parties therefore had to go through a cumbersome, 
complicated, and time-consuming procedure in order to 


219 


communicate their views. They also had to incur 
significant additional administrative and overhead 
expenses in order to pursue their right to free speech. 
While specific estimates on the costs incurred in 1996 
are not readily available, the administrative and over- 
head expenses associated with a separate party 
committee operation are substantial. As noted earlier 
(section III), the national party committees’ Hill com- 
mittees spend an average of 45 to 50 percent of their 
budgets on administrative and overhead expenses. 


Second, independent expenditures do not qualify for 
the lowest unit rates on the purchase of broadcasting 
time. Party coordinated expenditures do. Conse- 
quently, parties must spend more, in most cases almost 
twice as much more, for the same ad or message if it is 
financed independently instead of in a coordinated 
manner as allowed under §44la(d). Parties therefore 
have to spend more money for the same quantity of 
speech when they are speaking independently. A 
regulatory structure that encourages party committees 
to engage in independent expenditures thus increases 
the fundraising burdens on parties, since it puts them in 
a position where they have to spend more money to 
communicate with voters than they would if their 
speech was considered a coordinated expenditure. 


Third, when spending money independently, parties 
are required to separate themselves from their can- 
didates, at least with respect to any election activity 
associated with the independent expenditures. This is a 
less efficient and effective means of communicating 
with the electorate than under §441la(d). The best way 
for a party to help a candidate communicate with voters 
and develop an effective message is to work together in 


220) 


a coordinated manner. Such collaboration is not 
allowed with independent expenditures. 


A shift towards independent expenditures would be 
particularly undesirable because independent spending 
reduces the level of accountability in the system and is 
confusing to voters. Independent expenditures must be 
made without any coordination with candidates. The 
candidates they are intended to help, therefore, do not 
have to assume any responsibility for them. This is 
particularly true in the case of independent expendi- 
tures made against an opposing candidate. For 
example, if the Democratic party sponsors a message or 
ad against a Republican candidate that is not accurate 
or is controversial, the Democratic candidate can 
disavow association with the ad, claiming that he or she 
had no knowledge of the ad and therefore is not 
accountable for it. Such arguments, while based on legal 
reality, are confusing to voters, who view candidates as 
intimately connected with their parties, and don’t 
understand the legal distinctions between coordinated 
ads, independent ads, and candidate-sponsored ads. 
With coordinated expenditures, party spending is more 
closely related to the candidates who benefit from the 
activity, and the coordination between the party and 
candidates makes it easier to hold candidates responsi- 
ble for any spending. 


Coordinated spending therefore reflects a relation- 
ship between party and candidate that fits more closely 
with the way voters think about the election process. 
Electoral regulations should promote this relationship 
between parties and candidates, because it is a mean- 
ingful one for voters. Even though the share of the 
electorate that identifies with a political party has 


221 


declined in recent years, party identification is still the 
most important factor in helping voters shape their 
views of candidates. Party identification is a cue that 
helps voters distinguish candidates, frame policy 
choices, and make decisions on Election Day. Thus, by 
strengthening the ties between parties and candidates, 
campaign finance regulations can help improve. the 
accountability of the electoral process and make it 
easier for voters to hold parties and candidates respon- 
sible for their views. 


Independent expenditures are thus a less effective 
vehicle for party communications than coordinated 
expenditures, and are an inferior policy alternative. 
While the independent expenditure alternative allows 
parties to circumvent the limitations of §441la(d) and 
freely communicate with voters, it weakens the ties 
between a party and its candidates, and increase the 
fundraising demands party organizations must face. 
Further, independent expenditures raise the possibility 
of presenting the electorate with confusing messages 
about the relations between a party and its candidates, 
and threaten to reduce the level of accountability and 
responsibility achievable through the ballot box. 
Instead of promoting independent expenditures, the 
campaign finance system should encourage interaction 
between a party and its candidates by allowing parties 
to freely spend money on behalf of its party members 
who are seeking elective office. 


IV. Party Coordinated Expenditures and the First 
Amendment 


So political parties spend money on behalf of their 
candidates in an effort to help these candidates win 
elections, which is the principal goal of party organiza- 


90% 


tions in undertaking election-related activity. Because 
they are most interested in winning elections, parties 
spend these funds to help candidates gain voter sup- 
port. They are especially interested in making incum- 
bents in close races and non-incumbents more competi- 
tive, so that these party members will have a better 
chance of winning those contests that usually determine 
which party will constitute the majority in Congress. 
They pursue this end by helping communicate can- 
didates’ views to the electorate, in the belief that more 
effective and extensive communication will make voters 
more aware of a candidates’ attributes and policy posi- 
tions, and thus increase the share of the electorate 
willing to support those party members who are 
seeking elective office. Party expenditures on behalf of 
candidates are therefore an essential form of political 
speech. They are the preeminent means by which 
parties inform the electorate about their candidates 
seeking office, and they are a vital resource for chal- 
lengers, who often lack the funds needed to communi- 
cate effectively in their campaigns against well-known 
and well-financed incumbents. 


By prohibiting party organizations from spending 
more than a few cents per voter on behalf of their 
candidates, the FECA’s §44la(d) raises basic First 
Amendment concerns. This ceiling limits political 
speech because it severely restricts the amount of 
money parties may spend on behalf of their candidates. 
As the Supreme Court concluded in its Buckley deci- 
sion, a limit on the amount an individual or group can 
spend on political speech is no different from limiting 
political speech itself: “A restriction on the amount of 
money a person or group can spend on political com- 
munication during a campaign necessarily reduces the 


223 


quantity of expression by restricting the number of 
issues discussed, the depth of their exploration, and the 


size of the audience reached” (Buckley v. Valeo, 424 
U.S. 19). 


The limit on party coordinated spending particularly 
undermines First Amendment protections because 
these funds are the principal means by which parties 
expressly advocate the election or defeat of particular 
candidates. The general party funding provided under 
$431 in most cases cannot be used for this purpose. 
Moreover, the restrictions on spending under §441a(d) 
are so low that they do not allow many issues to be 
discussed or much depth of exploration. Indeed, the 
limits do not even allow the parties to conduct a mailing 
to all party members or conduct ad campaigns on a 
number of different issues. The ceilings thus impair the 
associational rights of party members because party 
organizations are not allowed to communicate freely 
with their members, a right that is granted to PAC 
members, labor union members, corporations, and other 
political groups under §431 of the FECA. The ceilings 
also impair the associational rights of party members 
by restricting the amount of speech they can jointly 
express to the public at large on behalf of their 
candidates. Since such speech is an essential means by 
which parties recruit new members and support, the 
limit also infringes on a party’s ability to attract new 
supporters. 


The Supreme Court has been very clear in setting a 
standard for judging such restrictions on political 
speech. Since its ruling in Buckley, the Court has held 
that preventing corruption or the appearance of corrup- 
tion are the only legitimate and compelling state 


> 4 


interests that justify restrictions on campaign finance. 
This standard has been upheld in all of the subsequent 
cases involving campaign spending restrictions that 
have come before the Court in the two decades since 
the Buckley opinion was issued. On this basis, the 
Court has struck down limits on the expenditure of 
personal funds by candidates seeking federal office, 
except in the case of presidential candidates receiving 
public funding; on the amount of campaign funds spent 
by congressional candidates; and on the amount of 
money spent independently by individuals, PACs, and 
other political groups. 


My understanding of the purposes of the FECA, 
based on my reading of the legislative history of the 
statute, is that the limitations on party spending under 
$441a(d) were not established to prevent corruption or 
the appearance of corruption in the electoral process. 
That purpose was served by the contribution limits 
placed on the funds parties could spend in connection 
with a federal election, as well as by the strict dis- 
closure requirements imposed on party funding gener- 
ally, and on party coordinated finances in particular. 
Instead, party spending was limited because Congress 
in the 1974 FECA was trying to control rising cam- 
paign costs and equalize the amounts candidates could 
spent on their campaigns for federal office. Congress 
recognized the close relationship between a party and 
its candidates, and understood that parties might spend 
money on behalf of their candidates to supplement the 
limited amounts the candidates themselves could spend. 
Congress therefore limited the amounts parties could 
spend on behalf of their candidates so that party 
spending would not become a means of circumventing 
the restraints imposed on candidate spending. Accord- 


225 


ing to the Court’s ruling in Buckley and subsequent 
cases, the purpose these limits are designed to 
serve—to equalize the resources available to candidates 
and control campaign spending—do not constitute a 
compelling interest that justifies restrictions on politi- 
cal speech. 


Moreover, a limit on the amounts parties spend on 
behalf of their candidates is not needed to prevent 
corruption or the appearance of corruption, because 
party coordinated expenditures are not corruptive nor 
do they create the appearance of corruption in the 
electoral process. Unlike PACs and other political 
groups, parties represent broad interests and diverse 
coalitions. Parties accumulate large numbers of 
contributions from a wide array of donors and interests, 
and pool these donations into a common fund that is 
used to finance all activities in connection with federal 
elections. Parties thus diffuse any real or perceived 
undue influence that might arise from any particular 
contribution. 


Indeed, a party’s membership and contributor base is 
so extensive that it cannot be controlled by any 
particular interest. Nor would a party even seek to be 
controlled by a few particular interests. The strength 
of a party organization depends on its ability to attract 
broad support among the electorate, an ability which 
would be hindered by adopting the perspectives of a 
single or even handful of special interests. Parties seek 
to appeal to majorities and to combine different 
interests into powerful electoral coalitions; they do not 
exist to advance narrow interests and try to curry 
legislative favors from members of Congress. For this 
reason, most campaign finance experts view party 


226 


money as the cleanest source of money in American 
politics. 


Furthermore, a party or its leadership and staff have 
no reason to corrupt public officials for economic 
benefit. As noted in the discussion of political parties in 
section I above, parties do not generate wealth from 
commercial activities or own independent financial 
enterprises that might benefit from particular legis- 
lative actions. Nor can they gain undue influence over 
government jobs or contracts, such “spoils” of the 
campaign finance system have long since been abol- 
ished and replaced with a civil service system, govern- 
ment procurement procedures, and a limited number of 
executive appointments that are determined by the 
President (in some instances with the advice and 
consent of the Senate), vot by the party leadership. 


For party funding to be corruptive, a two-step 
corruption process would have to occur. Initially, 
donors would have to corrupt or exert undue influence 
on the party organization or at least the party leader- 
ship. The parties, in turn, would have to corrupt the 
elected officials who represent the party in Congress 
and encourage them to perform quid pro quos for party 
donors. There is no evidence that such patterns of 
influence and decisionmaking take place as a result of 
party spending under §44la(d). 


There is no academic analysis or scholarly study 
conducted to date that demonstrates that parties are 
corrupted by the federally regulated contributions, the 
so-called “hard money funds,” they receive from donors. 
None of the studies of party finance or party coordi- 
nated spending contend that these funds are corruptive 
or generate the appearance of corruption in the political 


227 


process. Nor do these funds provide great opportunity 
for potential quid pro quos, since parties do not allocate 
these funds on the basis of a candidate’s position on any 
particular issue. 


What parties expect from their candidates is that . 
they will run effective campaigns and support party 
positions once elected, so long as the party’s view does 
not conflict with the individual’s personal views or 
constituency interests. Party committees therefore do 
not allocate party funds based on some issue litmus test 
or by comparing a candidate’s views to the views held 
by particular party donors. When parties are deciding 
whether to spend funds on behalf of a candidate, they 
chiefly examine the competitiveness of the district or 
race, the political situation of the incumbent, and the 
strength of the party contender’s candidacy. A can- 
didate’s ideology or positions on specific issues are not 
criteria for selecting which candidates to support, 
except in the rare instance where a candidate’s ideology 
might dramatically affect his or her competitiveness 
(e.g., a very liberal Democrat running in a conservative 
rural district with a large Republican registration 
advantage). And even in this rare instance, the party 
may decide to assist the candidate in an effort to pro- 
mote the party’s message in the district and thus build 
potential future support. But generally, the parties do 
not reward or punish candidates with party money in 
an effort to shape their beliefs. This basically “hands- 
off’ approach with respect to a candidate’s thinking is 
best summarized by the following comment by an 
NRCC official: 


At the NRCC, our only ideology is that you have a 
“big R” by your name. There is no litmus test on 


228 


any issue. We ask candidates how they feel on 
“issue X” and “issue Y.” Then, we help them articu- 
late what they feel. We are here to help Republi- 
cans win. (Cited in Herrnson, 1995:82) 


Staff members at the Democratic party committees 
express similar sentiments. 


If party committees were trying to use their 
expenditures to influence legislative behavior, one 
would expect that a significant share of the funding 
would be given to incumbent Representatives and 
Senators. But this is not the case. As the evidence 
from recent election cycles indicates, by all standard 
measures parties provide more funding to non- 
incumbents than incumbents. Whether considered in 
terms of the number of candidates to receive party 
funding, the total amount spent, or the average amount 
spent, most party money spent in coordination with 
candidates goes to non-incumbents. And almost none of 
the money spent by party committees goes to safe 
incumbents; when parties do assist an incumbent, it is 
usually because that member is in jeopardy of losing a 
bid for reelection. In these cases, the parties usually 
give to an incumbent because that seat is needed to 
help build a legislative majority, not because a 
particular donor or member of Congress has exerted 
undue pressure on party decisionmakers. 


Even if party leaders do try to use party funding as a 
means of encouraging party candidates or elected 
officials to support party positions, such legislative 
bargaining is a normal part of the coalition-building 
process in government. It is not a “corruptive” activity; 
it is not designed to give a private citizen or other 
entity some private advantage in exchange for a cam- 


229 


paign contribution. To argue that party leaders, by 
using the limited means available to them to try to 
encourage party members to support party positions, 
are engaging in corruptive quid pro quos that under- 
mine the integrity of the political process shows little 
understanding of the relationships between a party and 
its members who hold office, and of the process of 
coalition-building in modern legislatures. 


Parties and their candidates have a symbiotic, not 
corrupting, relationship. Party leaders have an interest 
in seeing their candidates succeed and in seeing the 
elected officials who sit in government under their 
banner serve their constituents well. Parties leaders 
also have an interest in trying to convince party mem- 
bers to support the broad positions parties advocate, 
since this is the way in which the parties help develop 
party cohesion in Congress and thereby encourage the 
formation of legislative majorities that are essential to 
the proper functioning of government. It is also a 
means by which a party helps fulfill the promises and 
preferences expressed in election campaigns, and thus 
upholds its responsibility to the voters who voted for its 
candidates. 


Studies of congressional voting behavior offer no evi- 
dence that votes of members of Congress or Senators 
are influenced by party coordinated expenditures or 
other party contributions. Scholarly research indicates 
that the factors that generally determine voting 
behavior are a member’s ideological predispositions and 
the interests of a candidate's political constituency, the 
voters he or she represents, not the influences of any 
particular campaign contribution (see, among others, 
Wright, 1997 and 1996; Herrnson, 1995; Grenzke, 1989; 


230 


Welch, 1982; Weisberg, 1978; Kingdon, 1973). The 
entreaties of party leaders or the provision or promise 
of party funding are certainly not important factors. 
Indeed, party leaders generally urge their members to 
support their constituent’s views, even if at the expense 
of the party position. Paul Herrnson describes this 
attitude as follows: “Recognizing the primacy of the 
electoral connection, party leaders generally tell 
legislators to respond to constituents rather than ‘toe 
the party line’ when the latter could endanger their 
chances of reelection” (Herrnson, 1995:234). Accord- 
ingly, party leaders have not made funding contingent 
on a candidate’s support for party positions. 


While most incumbents do not receive party funding, 
some incumbents do help raise money for the party. As 
with most party fundraising, elected officials are asked 
from time to time to assist their party with this 
necessary and difficult task. Party fundraising efforts 
to generate the monies needed to finance coordinated 
expenditures thus do offer another means by which a 
relationship between candidates and donors might be 
established. But again, there is little evidence to 
suggest that this help in raising party funds is corrup- 
tive or influences the voting behavior of members of 
Congress. Most of the money raised by party com- 
mittees is generated by means such as direct mail and 
fundraising events that do not involve direct solicitation 
by candidates or elected officials. The monies raised go 
to party financial accounts, not to the accounts of a 
particular candidate. Moreover, the law specifically 
prohibits elected officials or candidates from raising 
funds for the party and then earmarking them for use in 
connection with their own campaigns. And the data on 
party spending clearly show that monies received by 


231 


party committees are largely spent on non-incumbents, 
not on the incumbents who may ve in a position to help 
raise monies for party activities. 


Moreover, if the fundraising pressures on candidates 
were to be a concern, this would be another reason to 
question the current system of party spending limits. 
Because the parties are strictly limited with respect to 
the amounts they can spend on behalf of candidates, 
candidates cannot rely on party assistance to gain the 
resources they need to finance their campaigns. Con- 
sequently, candidates are forced to rely on their own 
fundraising efforts, which means that they have to 
spend more time raising money than they would under 
a system of unfettered party participation. 


Finally, the law already contains sufficient safe- 
guards to guard against any undue influence. All of the 
funds raised by the parties for use in connection with 
federal elections and all of the funds used to pay for 
coordinated expenditures are subject to federal contri- 
bution limits. Any individual contributions are subject 
to the $25,000 annual aggregate ceiling. The law 
prohibits the use of any labor treasury funds or corpo- 
rate funds. It also prohibits candidates and elected 
officials from raising contributions for the party and 
earmarking them for use in their campaigns. All of 
these monies are subject to strict public disclosure 
requirements. 


In addition, the parties must disclose all coordinated 
expenditures made on behalf of federal candidates. The 
parties must use a specific report form included in their 
federal disclosure reports to itemize all coordinated 
expenditures. For each expenditure, a party committee 
is required to report the name and address of the 


payee; the purpose of the expenditure; the name of the 
candidate benefitting from the expenditure, including 
campaign committee address and office sought; the date 
of the expenditure; the amount of the expenditure; and 
the aggregate amount the party has spent on behalf of 
that candidate. The data filed on these reports is sum- 
marized by the Federal Election Commission and re- 
ported in its press releases and reports. The Commis- 
sion provides both summary data on the amounts spent 
on behalf of House and Senate candidates by each 
party, as well as summaries for each candidate 
arranged by state and district. 


V. Conclusion 


Political parties differ from PACs or other political 
groups because they represent broad interests and 
coalitions of diverse viewpoints, rather than narrow 
interests with particular legislative concerns. Parties 
are therefore a unifying force in our pluralistic political 
society. They are an essential means of resolving 
differences between organized interests and large blocs 
of voters, and they work to develop ideologies and 
policy positions that will appeal to broad segments of 
the populace. 


Parties seek to attract supporters and expand their 
public appeal by promoting political discussion, deve- 
loping issue platforms, and, most importantly, par- 
ticipating in election campaigns. Election campaigns 
are the primary activity of party committees. Parties 
recruit candidates, assist candidates in organizing their 
campaigns, help them develop issue positions and com- 
municate their views to voters, and work to mobilize 
voter support. Parties take a great interest in helping 
their candidates contest elections, because candidate 


233 


speech is the principal means by which party views are 
disseminated to the electorate. Also, parties must win 
elections in order to build legislative majorities and see 
the policy preferences of their broad memberships 
translated into government action. 


Parties thus play an essential role in the electoral 
process. They are particularly important to the vitality 
of our democracy because they are the only source of 
funding available to federal candidates that favors 
challengers over incumbents. Unlike PACs and other 
organized interest groups, which are most concerned 
with achieving their particular legislative goals and 
thus support those candidates who reflect their particu- 
lar views, regardless of their party affiliation, parties 
are most concerned with helping their candidates run 
effective campaigns, irrespective of a candidate’s par- 
ticular issue positions or policy preferences. Con- 
sequently, PACs and other groups spend the vast 
majority of their political money on incumbents, while 
parties devote most of their resources to non-incum- 
bents, usually spending significant amounts on 
incumbents only when they are in jeopardy of losing 
their seats. Party funding thus helps to overcome the 
enormous financial advantage incumbents enjoy as a 
result of PAC donations and the benefits that accom- 
pany their positions in government. 


Political party spending therefore has a number of 
salutary effects on the political system. It reduces the 
influence of PAC or other interested money in the 
political system. It improves the candidate choices 
available to voters, enhances the level of corapetition in 
election contests, and encourages citizen participation 
in election campaigns. It also reduces the potential for 


254 


corruption in the system, since political parties are the 
cleanest source of money in the process and allocate 
resources without requiring candidates to take 
particular positions on issues. Indeed, no study of party 
coordinated spending has found that these monies have 
a corruptive effect on candidates or afford party donors 
undue influence in the legislative process. But these 
salutary effects, which primarily result from the coor- 
dinated expenditures that parties make on behalf of 
their candidates, are limited under current law, because 
the FECA strictly limits the amounts parties may 
spend in coordination with their candidates. 


The current ceiling on party coordinated spending 
under §441a(d) significantly weakens the role of parties 
in the political systems because it severely restricts 
their ability to communicate freely with their member- 
ship or the public at large. The ceiling prevents party 
committees from working with their candidates to 
communicate their views to the electorate in a 
meaningful way. For example, the ceiling is so low that 
it does not allow a party committee to produce and mail 
just one letter to the eligible party voters in a district, 
nor does it allow parties to broadcast more than one or 
two thirty-second ads to a statewide audience. Such 
stringent restraints significantly impede the ability of 
parties to expressly advocate the election of their 
candidates. 


The limit on party coordinated spending is particu- 
larly egregious because it serves no constitutional 
purpose. This statutory provision was not adopted to 
prevent corruption or the appearance of corruption in 
the political system; it was established to help cap 
campaign spending and equalize the resources available 


235 


to candidates—two objectives that have been overruled 
by the Supreme Court as justifying infringement on the - 
right of free speech. Moreover, in the two decades 
since the Court established the prevention of corrup- 
tion or the appearance of corruption as the only consti- 
tutional purpose for limiting political speech, no 
significant evidence has emerged that demonstrates 
that the federally-regulated monies spent by parties in 
connection with federal elections has a corruptive effect 
on the system, or even leads to an appearance of cor- 
ruption that justifies impairment of a party’s First 
Amendment rights. 


For these reasons, the limit on party coordinated 
expenditures under §441a(d) constitutes an unjustified 
limit on a party’s political speech and associational 
activities. It is also bad public policy, since it weakens 
the role of parties in the electoral process and increases 
the influence of alternative means of campaign finance 
that are not as beneficial as party finance.

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF COLORADO 
JUDGE EDWARD W. NOTTINGHAM 


Civil Action No. 89 N 1159 


FEDERAL ELECTION COMMISSION, 
PLAINTIFF/COUNTER-DEFENDANT 


v. 


COLORADO REPUBLICAN FEDERAL CAMPAIGN 
COMMITTEE, ET AL., DEFENDANTS/COUNTER-PLAINTIFF 


DECLARATION OF ROBERT HICKMOTT 


1. My name is Robert Hickmott. 


2. In 1980, I worked at the Democratic National 
Committee (DNC) as an Associate Finance Director 
during the Carter campaign. I did fundraising and 
advance work for the DNC, helping them raise money 
to make their 2 U.S.C. § 441la(d) coordinated party 
expenditures. 


3. In 1981, I became the Executive Director of a 
new DNC entity, the Democratic Business Council 
(DBC). The Democrats had just lost the White House, 
and the DBC was an attempt to be more aggressive in 
our outreach to solicit contributions from the business 
community. 


245 


4. Towards this end, the DNC set a new $10,000 
contribution level to join the DBC. Up to that time, it 
was $5,000. The $10,000 could include both corporate 
and non-corporate money. What contributors got in 
return was three or four weekend events throughout 
the country, in which they got together with party 
officials and Members of Congress to talk about issues, 
and to socialize. These were not fundraising events, but 
rather sustaining donor events where no additional 
money was raised. However, donors were asked to 
invite prospective donors, who might later decide to 


join the DBC. 


5. Potential donors were told of these events in 
advance, as an incentive to join the DBC. There was a 
brochure that spoke about meeting other like-minded 
business leaders, as well as Members uf Congress. For 
example, the Philadelphia Stock Exchange hosted DBC 
members at the Democratic Party’s 1982 mid-term 
convention, and different Members came by and 
discussed business and economic issues with donors. 
We also had dinners and receptions attended by 
Members of Congress. Every now and then we would 
go to a nice resort like Palm Springs or Nantucket, and 
Members of Congress would come and talk with donors. 
Most events attracted in the range of 35-50 donors, out 
of an eventual total of about 150 donors. 


6. We tried to match up donors’ interests with the 
Members who would be present. These were usually 
corporate donors who contributed for business pur- 
poses, so we tried to arrange, for example, that they 
could discuss tax issues with Sen. Bradley; or 
telecommunications issues with Rep. Wirth, when he 
was Chairman of the Telecommunications and Finance 


246 


Subcommittee. They also had opportunities to play golf 
with the Members, to have cocktails with them, or just 
to hang out together. 


7.  Iserved as National Finance Director for Tim 
Wirth’s Senate campaign from May 1985, through his 
election in Nov. 1986. We eventually raised over $4 
million. I served on Sen. Wirth’s Senate staff from Jan. 
1987 until Feb. 1989; and then, after a couple of years as 
a lawyer in private practice, I joined the Democratic 
Senatorial Campaign Committee (DSCC) in Jan. 1991 
as the Deputy Executive Director. However, the 
Executive Director and I also became de facto Finance 
Directors for the entire cycle and even, for a time, 
Political Directors, as well, after those holding the 
positions resigned. This was for the 1991-92 election 
cycle. 


s. As such, we were responsible for running the 
DSCC’s tally system during this cycle. The tally 
system goes back to at least when George Mitchell was 
Chairman [1985-86], and maybe further. It is an 
informal agreement between the DSCC and the 
candidates’ campaigns that if you help the DSCC raise 
contributions, we will turn around and help your 
campaign. 


9. Inthe 1986 Wirth Senate race, the DSCC could 
make over $100,000 in coordinated expenditures for our 
campaign, so there was pressure on the campaign, and 
on me, to raise this money for the DSCC. Sen. Mitchell, 
who was at that time the Chair of the DSCC, came out 
to Colorado. Norm Brownstein, a major Denver 
fundraiser, hosted a dinner and reception at which Sen. 
Mitchell was the featured guest. That and other 


247 


fundraising connected to the visit raised enough money 
for the DSCC to take care of our tally obligation, so that 
the DSCC would make the full amount of its permitted 
expenditures in support of our campaign. We also told 
contributors who had made the maximum allowable 
contribution to the Wirth campaign but who wanted to 
do more that they could raise money for the DSCC so 


that we could get our maximum § 441la(d) allocation 
from the DSCC. 


10. Mr. Brownstein was one of our campaign’s top 
fundraisers. He sponsored this event for the DSCC 
because it benefited the Wirth campaign. 


11. There are prescribed limits for coordinated party 
expenditures set out at 2 U.S.C. § 441a(d), which the 
Federal Election Commission computes and publishes 
early in each election cycle. At that time we know the 
legal limit the DSCC can expend in each Senate race. 
Because the DSCC does not have enough money to 
fully fund every candidate, they look at a number of 
factors when making allocations. These include the 
financial strength of the individual campaign, how much 
money they have been able to raise for themselves, 
what are the poll numbers, does the campaign have a 
chance to win, and how much money has the campaign 
raised for the DSCC. 


12. This situation was particularly acute in the 1991- 
92 cycle. Virtually every major State except Texas had 
a Senate election, plus we had two Senate races in 
California and a special election in Pennsylvania in 
1991, following the death of Sen. Heinz. 


248 


13. There is no set formula of “magic threshold” for 
how much money candidates are expected to raise 
before the DSCC will turn around and help them. 
However, we kept very detailed ledger sheets showing 
how much money was tallied foreach candidate. It got 
to the point where we talked to some campaigns on a 
daily basis, because they were so anxious to make sure 
that everything they were entitled to had been entered 
on their ledger sheet. When we got down to the 
allocation process, which traditionally begins after 
Labor Day, that was a key factor in deciding how much 
each Senate race was going to get in coordinated 
expenditures from the DSCC, 


14. Because this was so important, people would ask 
others to help out. We frequently faced the situation 
where several people wanted credit for the same 
contribution to the DSCC, such as, for example, the 
National Association of Homebuilders’ annual $15,000 
donation. So we developed the policy that the 
contributor would have to say in writing how the check 
should be credited. 


15. This system insured that the campaigns were 
very much on top of who contributed to the party 
committees. Early in each cycle, we held an 
incumbents’ retreat, where all of the incumbent 
Senators were told by the Chairman of their obligation 
to raise money for the DSCC. The Chairman and I also 
went around and visited each Senator, telling them 
what services the DSCC could provide, but also asking 
how they planned to raise money to meet their DSCC 
fundraising goal. 


249 


16. Incumbents are usually in a much better position 
to raise money that are challengers and those running 
for open seats. We did have a challengers’ retreat, 
which has a fundraising component but focused more on 
how to run a campaign and on the campaign services we 
could provide. 


17. We kept both Sen. Robb, who was DSCC Chair, 
and Sen. Mitchell, the Majority Leader, apprised as to 
how much tally money had been raised by the 
individual Senators. Because Sen. Robb could not focus 
too closely on the DSCC at the time, Sen. Mitchell was 
in many ways the de facto DSCC Chair and therefore 
had substantial influence on its operation. They kept on 
top of the Senators and admonished Senators who had 


not produced their fair share of contributions to the 
DSCC. 


18. Incumbents who were not raising money for 
themselves because they were not up for reelection 
would sometimes raise money for other Senators, or for 
challengers. They would send $20,000 to the DSCC and 
ask that this be entered on another candidate's tally. 
They might do this, for example, if they were planning 
to run for a leadership position and wanted to obtain 
the support of the Senators they assisted. This would 
personally benefit them, in addition to their doing their 
part to retain Democratic control of the Senate, which 
would preserve the legislative power of all Democratic 
Senators. While there was some talk of crediting part 
of this to their tally in a future cycle, we never did this, 
because we did not think the DSCC should be bound in 
subsequent cycles by money that had been raised and 
spent in a previous cycle. 


250 


19. The biggest problem with the tally system was 
that Senators and candidates wanted an ironclad 
contract that whatever money they raised for the 
DSCC would automatically be spent in support of their 
campaigns. That would be earmarking, which is illegal, 
and so we could not do it. 


20. It is obvious that a fledgling campaign that is 
struggling to meet its payroll, buy media, and so forth, 
and knows that the DSCC can allocate to them several 
hundred thousand dollars, wants to make certain that it 
will get back all of the money it raises for the DSCC. A 
lot of their local fundraisers and contributors hate the 
DSCC, they view it as a Washington fundraising entity, 
and want a guarantee that, if they make a $20,000 
contribution to the DSCC their candidate will see that 
money returned. 


21. We would insist that we review the fundraising 
letters that campaigns would send out, and would 
frequently have to rewrite them with respect to the 
tally process. While the campaigns did not like this, the 
only way the DSCC can hope to raise the amount of 
money it needs to spend is if the individual campaigns 
assist in raising a significant portion of the DSCC’s 
overall budget. 


22. The way the system operates, the bottom line 
has to be that some candidates get back more money 
than they raise, and others get back less. How much 
money a particular candidate received depended on the 
financial strength of the campaign itself, including both 
what they had raised on their own behalf and how much 
there was on the tally for the DSCC, and what their poll 
numbers looked like. 


251 


23. One thing we did across the board, for every 
campaign, was to give them a check for $ 17,500, the 
maximum direct contribution we were allowed to make 
under 2 U.S.C. § 44la(h). This had not been done in the 
past because of the feeling that some campaigns did not 
need it. However, we gave it to everyone. As I recall, 
we asked for this money back when Richard Shelby and 
Ben Nighthorse Campbell switched parties. 


24. Then the campaigns would start pleading with us 
for additional money. Early on, we would look at what 
their needs were, maybe an early poll or an early media 
buy, and we would fund those projects. When we got 
into the real allocation process, the DSCC’s Political 
Director, the Executive Director, and I would sit down 
and review exactly those factors I have noted above. 
We would then make recommendations to Sen. Robb 
and Sen. Mitchell for the allocation process. There was 
a group of other Senators, the Allocation Committee, 
involved as well. Sometimes they would approve our 
recommendations, but they might also increase them. 
Candidates know when there is going to be an 
allocation meting, and start lobbying members of the 
Allocation Committee, barraging them with news clips 
and poll numbers, arguing that an extra $200,000 would 
put them over the top. The Senators on the Allocation 
Committee would argue over who had the best chance 
of winning or who needed the money most. For 
example, we might have recommended spending a total 
of $750,000 at a particular time and the Allocation 
Committee would commit $900,000. 


25. Also, as it got closer to Election Day, when it 
seemed we could win every race, Senators would urge 
us to borrow an additional $2 million, promise faithfully 


to help us repay it, and then be nowhere to be seen 
after the election. That is why the DSCC winds up 
spending the first 6-8 months of each new cycle paying 
off debts of the previous cycle. 


26. The allocation process may have changed since I 
left. It is my understanding that Sen. Kerry, the 
current Chairman, makes most of these decisions 
unilaterally. 


27. Traditionally the DSCC was about one thing, 
raising money. In the mid-1980"s, they started getting 
into more areas, such as campaign services, strategy, 
and opposition research. They now have a staff 
between 25 and 35 people, most of whom are involved in 
fundraising. But there is a press operation, an opposi- 
tion research operation, and a political operation as 
well. 


28. During the election cycle I served on the DSCC, 
the political component of the DSCC would be in daily 
contact with the campaigns. They tried to know each 
campaign as well as an outsider can possibly know it. 
There were also constant visits by the Political Director 
and his or her staff, so they knew if the campaigns were 
(or were not) doing well. In my election cycle three 
people worked for the Political Director. They divided 
up the different campaigns by region and served as 
their campaigns’ contact at the DSCC. 


29. The DSCC contact advised the campaign 
whether it should be doing opposition research. In the 
case of a challenger, the DSCC might help the campaign 
understand what was happening in Washington on a 
legislative issue and how that could work to their 


253 


advantage as a challenger. The DSCC contact would 
also ask incumbent Senators to campaign for a certain 
challenger. 


30. We kept lists of fundraisers, pollsters, and media 
consultants that we made available to those who 
requested them. In one of the prior cycles we had a 
team of Democratic consultants who would fly into a 
campaign and try to help the campaign get up and 
running, the expenses of which would be allocated to 
that campaign. We pretty much let the campaign take 
the lead in how the money was spent. If we thought the 
money would be better used in other ways, we would 
tell them, but the final decision was theirs. They would 
send us their bills for the agreed upon amount, and we 
would pay them. 


41. I am not aware of any instance in which a 
Senator who had to cast a particularly difficult vote was 
promised DSCC money in return for that vote. On the 
other hand, I know the DSCC did not receive money 
from certain people or groups because Sen. Robb voted 
in favor of Clarence Thomas; and at times the labor 
community withheld money because they did not like 
some of his votes. While it is likely they did try to 
influence his vote on certain issues in exchange for 
their support of the DSCC, they would have done that 
through the normal legislative process, through 
lobbyists visiting his Senate office, and not through the 
DSCC. 


32. The Majority Leader decides who will Chair the 
DSCC. Consideration is given to who can raise money, 
as well as who is well thought of by contributors and 
has broad appeal to the business community. 


254 


33. The DSCC Chair is considered part of the Senate 
Leadership, and as such may have access to the Party's 
political agenda and be able to see how certain actions 
impact on the overall polities of the Democratic Party in 
the Senate. 


34. Approximately 50% to 60% of the DSCC’s money 
was raised by incumbent Senators and Senate 
candidates. We also had direct mail money and several 
sustaining donor programs. The Majority Trust was for 
$20,000 contributors (hard money). The Leadership 
Circle was for contributors of $15,000 in hard money, 
and was made up largely of PACs. There was also a 
Business Roundtable that was for $5,000 contributors. 


35. What contributors got for their contributions to 
the DSCC was similar to what we did years before at 
the DNC and the DBC. They would be invited to 
weekend events in which they would go to the Super- 
bow! or to Nantucket with Senators. They would 
socialize and discuss their interests and concerns. 
Donors paid their own travel costs to these events. The 
DSCC paid for the Senators’ travel, since we viewed 
this as an opportunity to spotlight candidates for 
potential contributors. We would also pick up the tab 
for the meal functions, or sometimes these were 
furnished as corporate in-kind contributions. 


36. Some contributors made part of their required 
$15,000 or $20,000 contribution by raising money for the 
party from others or by attributing the cost of hosting a 
Sustaining Donor event in their homes against their 
annual contribution to the DSCC. We could encourage 
contributors to recruit other potential contributors by 
inviting them to attend. Then we would contact these 


255 


people and solicit them for a contribution, hoping they 
would be interested in the opportunity to rub elbows 
with the Senators and party officials. 


37. We were at times able to combine fundraising 
projects. For example, if we were holding a Majority 
Trust weekend in Nantucket, some Senators might stop 
in Boston on the way and do a tally event for Sen. 
Kerry’s campaign. 


38. We also had monthly breakfasts featuring a 
guest speaker, such as a Senator who was of interest to 
the contributors. Sen. Robb would usually give an 
update on the different races at these breakfasts. 


39. The larger the contribution, the smaller and 
more exclusive the group of people who attended the 
get togethers with the Senators. For example, at the 
Democratic Convention in New York in 1992, the 
Majority Trust got better seats in Madison Square 
Garden, and I think they also got a luncheon with Sen. 
Mitchell and the Democratic Leadership. This was 
smaller and more intimate than the Leadership Circle 


functions, which were smaller than the $5,000 donor 
functions. 


40. The annual DSCC dinner is at the other end of 
the spectrum. It is a huge event, and each Senator is 
told he or she has to sell tickets for a certain number of 
tables at $20,000 a table. A Senator might go to a 
prominent lobbyist and ask for help in raising this 
money from the lobbyist’s clients. Later, the Senator 
might remember that the lobbyist had helped him or 
her raise this money, at the time that the lobbyist’s 


256 


clients needed the Senator’s help with a legislative 
matter. 


41. Early in each election cycle the DSCC would 
routinely contact the State Democratic Party 
committees in all states in which we had Senate 
candidates, and ask that they transfer their coordinated 
expenditure limit (“§ 441a(d) authority”) to us. They 
never to my knowledge asked for anything in return. 
No state ever refused to hand over its § 44la(d) 
authority or even tried to give us only partial authori- 
zation. On the contrary, they appeared to be relieved 
to be rid of their obligation to raise hard dollars to 
spend on their Senate candidates. Occasionally, if we 
could not fully fund a race, we would return that 
authority to the state party at the state party’s request, 
so they could try to raise and spend the full amount 
authorized by law. 


42. In the final stages of the 1992 election, we had a 
lot of coordination with the DNC and the Clinton/Gore 
campaign, to the point that we were making 
recommendations to them as to where an infusion of 
their money would help our Senate candidates. This 
was get out the vote (“GOTV”) money. Typically, 
though, we did not have a close working relationship 
with them, or with the DCCC. This may have changed 
now that the DNC has a lot more money than it used to. 


43. Nor did we have much to do with the co- 
ordinated campaigns for state candidates run by the 
individual state party committees, became back then 
we raised very little soft money. We might instruct 
challengers how to conduct a GOTV campaign, but that 
was tutorial, rather than part of our budgeting and 


257 


fundraising operation. Again, this may have changed 
since soft money 1s a much larger component of the 
DSCC’s expenditures than when I was there. 


44. We had very little contact with the state party 
committees at all. Most of our dealings were directly 
with the Senate campaigns, and those campaigns then 
dealt with their state parties. Most state parties were 
so anemic and ineffective that, from our point of view 
there was little to be gained from direct interaction. 
There were a few that had enough resources to have 
some impact, such as the California and New York state 
party committees, but not very many. 


45. This was also true of Sen. Wirth’s 1986 race. We 
were such a cash-starved campaign that we were fully 
preoccupied with raising money for the campaign 
rather than for the State Party. There was some 
orchestration between the State Party and the Wirth 
campaign as far as the things they could do for us under 
the law, such as generic party building and GOTV, but 
that was handled by the State Party Chairman and the 


Wirth Campaign Manager in Colorado, while I re- 
mained in Washington. 


46. There is a very rare strata of contributors who 
contribute large amounts to the DSCC because they 
actually believe in Democratic politics and the fact that 
the Democratic Party represents something that the 
Republican Party does not. The majority of those who 
contribute to political parties do so for business 
reasons, to gain access to influential Members of Con- 
gress and to get to know new Members. 


258 


47. If you want to get to know Members of Con- 
gress, or new Members of Congress, it is more efficient 
to write a $15,000 check to the DSCC and to get the 
opportunity to meet them at our various events than it 
would be to write fifteen $1,000 checks to fifteen 
different Senators, or Senators and candidates. 


48. This can ultimately prove useful when the 
contributor calls up and reminds a Senator that he met 
him in Minneapolis at the Superbowl, and says he would 
like to come in and discuss pending legislation. The 
door is more likely to be open to him than if the call was 
from a non-contributor. 


49. On occasion a contributor would call the DSCC 
and ask us to arrange a meeting with a Democratic 
Senator, and we would try to comply. We maintained 
good relationships with the Administrative Assistants 
{[AA] in the Senators’ offices; and, while we would not 
say you must do this, we would spell out the circum- 
stances in seeking agreement to meet with the con- 
tributor. Since we did not deal in legislation at the 
DSCC but were purely a fundraising committee, we 
might not know if there was something going on that 
would make a particular meeting inappropriate. If so, 
the AA might tell us it wasn’t the right thing to do at 
the time. 


50. There were some contributors who expected 
something further for their money and did not like 
being told they could not have this meeting arranged. 
No one ever asked for their money back in that 
situation, although some threatened not to contribute in 
future years. Since we were an incumbents’ committee, 


259 


we felt an obligation to protect the Senators from being 
placed in a potentially awkward situation. 


51. A lot of our big contributors were contributing to 
the Republicans as well. If you look at our parse 
Circle, which is predominantly $15,000 PAC rane 
butors, there is a high correlation between those who 
give money to the DSCC and those who give money to 
the Republicans. This was less likely to be true of 
Majority Trust members. who were mostly business 
people who were Democrats. thi 

~s) 


JG. 


7 oe a contributor would give money to 
: . wn U C in lieu of giving to a candidate. For example 
if'a candidate did not take PAC money, the PAC might 


donate to the DSCC inste: : 
: . ‘nstead and have it tal 
candidate's account. anata 


o3. I think there ought to be limits on what parties 
can spend on elections. It is hard to quantify a direct 
correlation between iarge contributions and corruption 
or appearance of corruption, in the political process On 
the other hand, recent allegations about large amounts 
of money flowing into the DNC or the Clinton/Gore 
White House certainly give the perception that there is 
4 correlation between access and large contributions 
Both parties sponsor Sustaining Donor programs, su ch 
as those we have been discussing, for which the fa 
committees sponsored events where large contributors 
would meet with Senators. Increasing the amount of 
money that 4 committees can spend on campaigns 
will necessar¥*A—rease the pressure to raise money 


through such events. 


260 


54. Obviously I would have liked to have had more 
money when I was with the DSCC. We lost several 
races by just a few percentage points. However, I 
think the system is prone to corruption. If unlimited 
amounts of money can be spent, and this is not properly 
policed, a national party committee could overwhelm a 
Senate race with money. We keep ratcheting up the 
limits. and there is no telling where this will end. 


55. This all comes back to the allocation system. 


This is a decision made by Democratic Senators. If 


people start giving $50,000, $100,000, or $500,000 to a 
party committee, even if that is soft money, they would 
probably expect a greater voice in how that money Was 
spent and what they would want in return. It is 
unrealistic to think that if I give you a check for 
500,000, I will not expect you to make the largest 
possible contribution to my favorite candidate who can 
do the most for my legislative concerns. 


56. Permitting such large eontributions would also 
discourage participation by those who can only make 
small contributions. People already think the system is 
se out of whack, they ask, why should I give a check for 
$25 or $50, because that doesn’t really mean anything. 
The direct mail proceeds for all three Democratic 
national committees have gone way down, and these 
donors are the “true believers.” Many of them are re- 
tired. and are concerned about issues like Social Se- 
curity and liberal philosophical issues. ] think their 
participation is an important aspect of democracy, and 
it would be a shame if they stopped contributing be- 
cause they feel their small checks have no impact. 


261

4. Sen. Ford chaired the Democratic Senatorial 
Campaign Committee (DSCC) in the 1981-1982 election 
eyele. Sen. Mitchell held this position in the 1985-1986 
cycle. My experience was that both Senators kept their 
DSCC work separate from the office, in the sense that 
no legislative staff was invelved with DSCC campaign 
activities. I had no direct involvement in DSCC work, 
although I did gain general knowledge about DSCC 
operations from my proximity to the Chairmen. 
Additionally, my extensive work from 1987 through 
1994 on campaign finance reform legislative efforts 
exposed me to DSCC issues. It is my sense that 
Administrative Assistants know who has contributed 
both to their Senator’s campaign and to the DSCC, 
although some of them keep closer track of this than 
others. 
>. At least on the Democratic side, the Majority

9 It is only natural that most individuals and 
PACs want to make sure that they get a 
recognition for whatever money they contribute. } 
course, some expect nothing In return, while others - 
more aggressive in trying to get a lot of mileage ont - 
their contributions. I would expect most of the largest 
PACs give the maximum allowed, or close to it, to party

265 


10. Contributors believe this interaction has an 
effect on Members, whether it does or not. Certainly, it 
does have an effect on some Members. If you are 
raising money for your campaign, or you have a tally 
and you're raising money for the party, and you have 
solicited a $15,000 or $20,000 contribution, oftentimes 
this is going to have some effect. This is just based on 
human relationships, you are grateful to people who 


want to help you and naturally want to be responsive to 
them. 


11. The bottom line is that there is a symbiotic 
relationship between contributors and candidates. A 
contributor gives to have the opportunity to influence a 
Member, and the Member is willing to provide this 
opportunity in order to raise more money. While this is 
usually subtle, it can be extremely direct. For example, 
I know of a company that was recently involved in a 
legislative battle critical to its bottom line. One Senator 
in particular was working on this issue. The company, 
which was known to be a financial supporter of the 
other party, was asked by the Senator, “Well, where 
have you been?” and the Senator’s representatives 
later told the company that they had to “be at the 
table,” to contribute, if they wanted their views to be 
heard. This was a shakedown, pure and simple. And it 
worked -— the company wound up contributing to the 
national Senatorial Campaign Committee of the 
Senator’s party, as requested. 


12. Very little money that does not come through 
the mail is what I would call “detached” money. 
Typically, a contributor gives money to establish 
relationships, to be able to lobby on an issue, to get 
close to Members, to be able to have influence. While 
an elected official of course does not have to do 
something because somebody gave, a contribution helps 


266 


establish a — oy ~~ — fa f hy 

the relationship. is tie

lot of money. Rather, it Is one —_ — - ~ 
relationships are established because peop e gv io

because you have established a relationship.

DECLARATION OF THE HON. PAUL SIMON 


1. My name is Paul Simon. I represented the 24th 
Congressional District of Illinois in the U.S. House of 
Representatives between 1975-1985, and served as a 
U.S. Senator from Illinois from 1985 to 1997. 


2. While serving in the House, I made phone calls 
to raise money for the Democratic Congressional Cam- 
paign Committee (DCCC). I also attended their 
fundraising events, both in the Washington area and in 
other cities. After I moved to the Senate, I did much 
the same thing, on a larger scale, for the Democratic 
Senatorial Campaign Committee (DSCC), 


3. While some of the party committee events | 
attended were large affairs, most had 20-25 people and 
were held in somebody's home. They usually were held 


268 


for the benefit of a specific Senate campaign as ve as 
for the party committee itself. Each House — Y vd 
Senator present would say a few words, aS W on d ~ 
Chair of the DCCC or the DSCC, and then we — 
socialize with the donors. About once a year I attende , 
an all-day conference for major donors where reer ne 
would each speak about legislation we were intereste 


in, and answer questions. 


4. The DCCC and the DSCC would ask Members te 
make phone calls seeking contributions to the ree 
They would assign me a list of names, people | es 
known previously, and I would just go down the ist. 
am certain they did this because they found it more 
effective to have Members make calls. However, = 
phone manner is rather low key, lower key than the 


committees like. 


5. 1 tried to avoid asking donors who had _ 
viously given to my campaigns to contribute - - : 
party committees, because I didn’t want to ov - 0! 1 
welcome. However, I did co-host party fundraising 
events in Illinois, where invitations were likely sent to 
my contributors. 


6. If this were a DSCC event, the money raised 
would be credited to Senate candidates based on the 
DSCC’s tally system. Lonors would be told the i 
they contributed could be credited to any Senate — a 
date. The callers would make clear that this was no J 
direct contribution, but it was fairly close to west. ~ 
contributors would have the sense that this wou 
benefit my campaign, if they contributed to the party. 


269 


7. While I don’t believe we were given a specific 
fundraising goal, we knew how much the DSCC could 
contribute in Illinois, which in my campaigns was a 
little more than $1,000,000. It was recognized that the 
closer you came to that with your tally, the more likely 
you were to get the full $1,000,000. 


8. With regard to the Illinois Democratic Party, my 
own experience was that I more frequently got 
requests from them for money than the other way 
around. I sometimes made phone calls for them, or they 
would request a check for $5,000 to help with get out 
the vote activities, or a gubernatorial race. | may also 
have appeared at fundraising events for the State 
Party. 


9. However, I generally did not work closely with 
the State Party on anything, because I never regarded 
them as major players. This has been true historically 
in Illinois, although it may be different in other States. 
It may also be different in Illinois, with regard to state 


races. But they were not a significant factor in my 
Senate races. 


10. It is not unusual for large contributors to seek 
legislative favors in exchange for their contributions. A 
good example of that which stands out in my mind 
because it was so stark and recent occurred on the next 
to last day of the 1995-96 legislative session. Federal 
Express wanted to amend a bill being considered by a 
Conference Committee, to shift coverage of their truck 
drivers from the National Labor Relations Act to the 
Railway Act, which includes airlines, pilots and 
railroads. This was clearly of benefit to Federal 
Express, which according to published reports had 


270 


contributed $1.4 million in the last 2-year cycle to 
incumbent Members of Congress. 


11. I opposed this in the Democratic Caucus, arguing 
that even if it was good legislation, it should not be 
approved without holding a hearing, we should not cave 
in to special interests. One of my senior colleagues got 
up and said, “I’m tired of Paul always talking about 
special interests; we've got to pay attention to who is 
buttering our bread.” I will never forget that. This 
was a clear example of donors getting their way, not on 
the merits of the legislation, but just because they had 
been big contributors. I do not think there is any 
question that this is the reason it passed. 


12. I believe people contribute to party committees 
on both sides of the aisle for the same reason that 
Federal Express does, because_ they want favors. 
There is an expectation that giving to party committees 
helps you legislatively. 


13. Giving to party committees also helps you gain 
access to Members. While I realize some people argue 
donors don't buy favors, they buy access, that access is 
the abuse, and it affects all of us. If I got toa Chicago 
hotel at midnight, when I was in the Senate, and there 
were 20 phone calls waiting for me, 19 of them names I 
didn’t recognize and the 20th someone I recognized as a 
$1,000 donor to my campaign, that is the one person I 
would call. You feel a sense of gratitude for their 
support. 


14. The fact that big donors have access gives them 
a huge leg up in the process. In a very real sense, we 
are going through the old fight between Thomas 


271
