DECLARATION OF FORMER SENATOR 
TIMOTHY E. WIRTH 


1. lam Timothy E. Wirth, Undersecretary of State 
for Global Affairs. I completed graduate studies in 1973 
and also held appointments in the executive branch of 
the federal government from 1967-70. 


2. lama former Member of Congress (“Member”). 
In 1974, I was elected to the U.S. House of Representa- 
tives (“House) from the Second ny are pon 

e State of Colorado (“second district”). - 
pb this District through 1986. From 1987 through 
1992, I served as a Senator for the State of Colorado in 
the U.S. Senate (“Senate”). In 1992, I decided not to 
run again for the U.S. Senate. During my — 
mately 18 years of Congressional service, | campaign 


273 


for election or for re-election to federal office seven (7) 
times. 


3. During my election campaigns, I raised funds for 
the Democratic Party to support my campaign. 


4. By the early 1980s, | understood that the 
Colorado Democratic State Party was becoming pro- 
gressively less capable of raising funds. Because I was 
successful at fundraising and could reach various consti- 
tuencies effectively, I worked to raise funds for the 
State party. 


5. When I solicited contributions for the state 
party, in effect I solicited funds for my election 
campaign. I understood that the solicitees who made 
contributions to the party almost always did so because 
they expected that the contributions would Support my 
campaign one way or another, and for the most pat 
they expected I would remember their contributions. 


6. The chairman of the state party served at the 
pleasure of Democratic elected officials. He knew the 
amount of funds I had raised for the state party, and 
this helped me to participate in deciding how the party 
would spend its funds. For example, the party used 
some of the funds to finance a Get Out The Vote 
campaign (“GOTV”) for the second district when | 
campaigned for the House seat, and for the entire state 
when I ran for the Senate seat in 1986. During the time 
I was in Congress, we tried to persuade other Demo- 
cratic candidates for House seats in other congressional 
districts in Colorado to raise funds for the state party, 
to enable the party to finance similar GOTV campaigns 
in their respective districts. These coordinated GOTV 


274 


campaigns were important for Democratic candidates 
at all levels, and when properly operated, gave some 
leadership to the Members of Congress, and helped to 
knit together a naturally disparate party. 


7. Although most of my experience in fundraising 
for the Democratic Party was for the state party, I also 
successfully solicited funds for a national party 
committee, the Democratic Senatorial Campaign 
Committee (“DSCC”). The DSCC required that I raise 
a certain amount of money for the committee before my 
campaign could receive DSCC funds. I knew how much 
I needed to raise because the DSCC applied a formula, 
depending on the size of a particular candidate’s state, 
for fundraising and for expending funds in a race that it 
considered “marginal”. A “marginal” race was one in 
which the candidate received 55 percent, or less, of the 
vote in the preceding election. 


8 | understood that when I raised funds for the 
DSCC, the donors expected that I would receive the 
amount of their donations multiplied by a certain 
number that the DSCC had determined in advance, 
assuming the DSCC had raised other funds. For 
example, a donor might expect that for every dollar 
contributed to the DSCC I would receive $3 from the 


DSCC. 


9 In this fundraising, I often solicited contributions 
to the DSCC from individuals or Political Action Com- 
mittees (PACs) who already had “maxed” (contributed 
to my campaign committee the maximum amount 
allowed by federal law). I did so because as J] remem- 
ber, individual solicitees were allowed by law to 
contribute $20,000 to the DSCC in a calendar year,

14. From time to time I introduced large donors to 
the national party committees. The Democratic Party

wanted to become acquainted with party leaders. The

19. I am aware of the print and broadcast media's

by large donors to the party.

Do you believe that the million dollar authority

A. Well, the answer is I have never promised anyone 
anything for any campaign.

A. They would know me well enough to know that if

third, that puts somebody under appreciable depen- 
dence and you are—I think you are getting on to very

Whether you get on the thin ice when you go from 
a million to a million and a half, I can’t quantify it

Q. You believe that the Democratic Committee

A. I think they would be getting on dangerous ice

Q. You don’t recall any of your fellow senators

A. I do not. 


Q. What about individual contributors to your 
campaign? Did you ever encounter quid pro quo 


demands from individual contributors? 


A. The only time I can recall it being completely - 
this goes back to when I was in the state legislature. 


Some cemetery group, of all people, came to me 
and said they had contributed $200 to my campaign and 
I voted against some legislation they wanted on 
headstones. I can’t remember any of the details. I 
offered to return the $200. They declined that. That’s 
the only time. 


Now, I have had people say to me “We were good 
to you in the last campaign. We would sure appreciate 
your vote on this amendment or this bill.” It is not very 
subtle.

A. I think you are correct, but I think you 
have to be careful as you make that assumption. 


I think if money goes to the party and then from the 
party to me there is a little bit of a safeguard. 


But we have a situation - 1 happen to be co-chairing 
together with Bill Stratton, former Republican

the whole campaign finance. 


Illinois has the weakest campaign finance laws of all

campaign contributions and I want to pay for my 
daughter’s college tuition, you can do it in Illinois. 


In Illinois the party leaders, the legislative leaders — 
I mentioned-today the state party being weak in 
Illinois. The state party is strong in Illinois in terms of 
the state legislature, not in terms of the United States 
Senate.

Is that fair? 

A. Yes. I think that is fair. I don’t think that is

Q. While you were the head of the DCCC, did you 
ever persuade members to support the interests of big 
donors because they were big donors? 


A. No. 


Q. During the time that you were head of the DCCC, 
did you ever go to members and tell them you have to 
vote this way because that is what this big donor for 
the party wants you to do? 


A. No, sir. 


Q. During the time that you were head of the DCCC, 
did you have the power to coerce members to vote to 
support the interests of the big donors if you wanted to 
do it? 


A. That is a hard question to answer. 


I think I would say no, but I think people could 
interpret by not being willing to support somebody in a 
committee assignment or not willing to target a political 
race, that the potential abuse is there and this power, in 


2388 


the hands of the wrong person, could be used nega- 
tively. 


The answer to that question is in the wrong hands 
it could easily be yes, but, again, it goes back to my 
discussion earlier about the baseball game. I don’t 
think that you can legislate to try to prevent the 
tyrannical leader from abusing the system. | think you 
have to permit a system that should function and 
hopefully, like in the case of Joe Cannon, the members 
rebelled and kicked the person out when they abused 
the process. 


Q. You anticipated my question. 


Are there mechanisms within the party for 
responding to such an abuse of power by a party 
leader? 


A.Yes. And I would use a current situation to 
discuss it. 


When Newt Gingrich got in trouble and had 11 
members go against the leadership, Newt publicly said 
that he was going to run people against these people in 
the Republican primary. 


Major blunder in my view because back home 
these individuals became heroes and instead of 
defeating these individuals, Newt was in effect creating 


independent power centers, which as a party leader you . 


can’t have. 


Within two weeks he has withdrawn from that 
position and has directed the campaign chair to say 
there will not be a campaign against these individuals 
and there will not be an attempt to discipline. 


289 


We always had that difficult 
, y. I had people in the 
leadership every time somebody wouldn’t ak auote, 


“the party position”, there were i 
I "; people in our part 
who wanted to retaliate against them. ey, 


I always point out the famous example of what 
really helped us to punish Phil Graham by kicking him 
off the Budget Committee so that he quit his district 
and ran as a Republican and now is the United States 
Senator in the State of Texas and has made Texas into 
a competitive political state. If we had been wiser and 
more patient, Phil may still be a Democrat today. 


I think you have to be careful. I think it can 
boomerang against you very, very easily and I have 
seen a number of occasions where a leadership has
